## Blazing Star <note>original: "flammende Stern"—a central symbol in Freemasonry representing divine providence or the light of reason; it is also the title of a famous polemical Masonic work by Baron de Tschoudy.</note>

### Diversity of Opinions Regarding the Origin of Freemasonry.

Suppose an ignorant and insolent charlatan thinks meanly enough of his listeners to recite obvious absurdities to them in the tone of an enthusiast <note>In the 18th-century context, an "enthusiast" often referred to someone claiming direct divine inspiration or religious ecstasy, often used pejoratively here to mean a fanatic.</note>; suppose he is bold enough, in order to give weight to his cause, to present the **Masonic art**—in those speeches intended for the persuasion of the mind and the improvement of the heart—as an eternal, and indeed a necessarily eternal science; what can come of it? Doubtless, science <note>In this context, "Wissenschaft" refers to a systematic body of knowledge or wisdom.</note> is in God, it is always and constantly in Him: a pious and sound philosophy can reason about everything in such a way; but this sublime metaphysics regarding the perfections of the Supreme Being goes

<summary>This page introduces a critical discussion on the origins of Freemasonry, warning against "charlatans" who make exaggerated claims about the craft being an "eternal science." It sets a philosophical tone, distinguishing between divine wisdom and the actual history of the Masonic order.</summary>

<keywords>Freemasonry, Blazing Star, Masonic art, metaphysics, Grand Lodge of Hungary, Baron de Tschoudy, origins of Masonry</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory argument from the previous page, which questioned the metaphysical claims of those who trace Freemasonry’s origins to a divine, eternal source. The text here shifts to a critique of allegorical interpretations of scripture and the human tendency to mistake antiquity for inherent value.</meta>

has nothing to do with those historical truths, which one must take from time and determine by a fixed epoch <note>The author argues that while God is eternal, human institutions like Freemasonry are historical and must be dated to a specific era rather than claimed as eternal.</note>. I am well aware that in some passages of the **sacred books**, God is presented with a **trowel** <term>trowel: a primary tool of the operative mason, used in speculative Freemasonry to represent the spreading of the cement of brotherly love</term> in his hand, as he issues his orders from the battlements of the walls of **holy Zion** <note>original: "heiligen Zions"—referring to Jerusalem and specifically the Temple of Solomon, a central motif in Masonic legend</note>, organizes the work, joins stones together, and binds them with the proper mortar; but does this metaphor, which is found almost exclusively in mostly allegorical writings, not belong among those obscure parables whose meaning is purely moral, and with which one need not concern oneself with much over-reasoning? Exaggerated bombast and high-flown boasting often border so closely on nothingness that the fate of such a **hypothesis** is easily foreseen. More skillful, and perhaps more dangerous people—because they possessed greater skill in persuasion and knew better how to grasp the weak sides of humanity—have dared to assert more tolerable fables.

Vanity assumes as a principle that the more something bears the stamp of **antiquity** at its core, the more it proves greatness and merit: the general applause, which gladly turns toward the miraculous

<meta>catchword: -miraculous</meta>

<summary>The author critiques the literal interpretation of biblical metaphors that depict God as a mason, arguing that Freemasonry's origins should be sought in historical epochs rather than allegorical myths. He warns that human vanity often conflates the antiquity of a tradition with its actual value or truth.</summary>

<keywords>historical truth, allegory, Zion, Masonic trowel, antiquity, critique of origins, vanity, biblical metaphor</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s skeptical examination of how human vanity values things based on their age. It transitions from a critique of philosophical exile and censorship to a discussion of how various European orders and societies—such as the Knights of Saint John—claim ancient lineages to gain social prestige.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<sig>A 2</sig>

...tends toward the [miraculous], has sanctified this principle: just as if a river, which loses itself in the immeasurable ocean, were ennobled fifty miles from its source. Yet, let us grant some merit to the opinion once it has been accepted; the wise man knows well what he must hold to, but admittedly, not every **philosopher** <term>philosopher: here referring to an Enlightenment thinker who uses reason to critique established traditions and dogmas</term> is permitted to speak freely: one must first have been driven from two-thirds of **Europe** <note>original: "Europens"—The author likely refers to the common 18th-century experience of radical thinkers like Voltaire or Rousseau, who faced exile for their writings</note>, only to be allowed—in that small corner where one lives in poverty, and where the watchful **police** <term>Policey: in this period, the term referred to the general administration, social regulation, and censorship apparatus of a state</term> will not leave one in peace for long—to instruct and combat the human race. Good and sound logic allows for the adoption of certain **hypotheses**: let us therefore assume that, in truth, the oldest **family tree**, the most distant origin, is also the most glorious; thus, we should no longer be surprised that many **orders** and **societies** have taken such great care to glorify themselves through turgid narratives of their far-reaching lineage.

I shall not go through all the various societies spread throughout Europe; many deserve our respect and reverence, almost all have the applause of the Great and rely on their authority: **Hospitallers** <term>Hospitaliten: members of a medieval military and religious order founded to provide care for poor, sick, or injured pilgrims to the Holy Land</term>, **Knights of Saint John** <term>Johanniter: the German name for the Order of St. John; the author is likely listing these to contrast their known history with the more "mythical" histories of other secret societies</term>, even

<summary>The author critiques the logical fallacy that a society's value is derived from its antiquity and reflects on the censorship faced by Enlightenment thinkers who challenge such traditions. He begins to list established European orders that use their long histories and the support of the nobility to maintain their prestige.</summary>

<keywords>antiquity, Enlightenment, censorship, social lineage, Knights Hospitaller, Knights of Saint John, philosophical skepticism</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of established European orders—such as religious and knightly foundations—before pivoting to a mysterious "secret society." The previous page ended with a mention of "Hospitalers" (the Knights of Saint John), leading into the discussion of monks and chivalric orders on this page.</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>

...even monks; in all of them one sees a useful and venerable purpose; all are confirmed, recognized, and protected; all of them—if one only examines the purpose of their initial foundation closely—show themselves from a favorable side, and are indeed truly beneficial. They are schools for heroes, **nurseries** <term>Pflanzschulen: literally "planting schools," a common 18th-century metaphor for institutions intended to "cultivate" or train specific types of people, like soldiers or scholars</term> of great men, rewards for warriors, a refuge for the needy nobility, dwellings of virtue and good deeds, residences for the perfection of **morality** <term>Moral: in this context, it refers not just to personal ethics, but to the social conduct and character expected of the upper classes</term>, and its practice and application. I am content to recognize the useful and happy success of such connected societies, without needing to know exactly what prompted their origin. Thus, we are accustomed to highly esteeming the **Garter** <note>original: "Hosenband"—Referring to the Order of the Garter, the highest order of British knighthood</note> worn by a **Great Lord** <note>original: "an einem Großen"—Referring to a member of the high nobility or a person of significant rank</note> as a sign of special honor, without thinking of the dull and ambiguous **jest** <note>The author refers to the legend that King Edward III founded the Order of the Garter after the Countess of Salisbury lost her garter during a dance; the King picked it up to spare her shame, uttering the famous phrase "Honi soit qui mal y pense" (Shame on him who thinks evil of it)</note> that gave occasion to the creation of this famous order.

We are speaking here of a secret and special society, which grows daily, has existed for a long time, is always under suspicion, and is sometimes persecuted: a society that has everything for itself internally, yet everything **against it** <note>The OCR reads "wieder sich," which is an archaic spelling of "wider sich" (against itself/opposed)</note> externally; in which one might [find] <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the catchword from the original manuscript indicates the next page begins with "finden" (to find)</note> the seed of all others.

<summary>The author argues that established religious and knightly orders serve noble social purposes regardless of their potentially trivial origins, then introduces a mysterious, growing, and often persecuted secret society.</summary>
<keywords>monastic orders, chivalry, Order of the Garter, social utility, secret societies, virtue, nobility</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument from page 4. After discussing the various recognized knightly and religious orders of Europe, the author transitions into a discussion of a specific, more mysterious society—Freemasonry—suggesting that while its name might seem unremarkable, its moral purpose is to refine and select the best members of society. This page ends with the beginning of a philosophical or mythological exploration of the society's origins, starting with the figure of Adam.</meta>

<page-num>5</page-num>
<sig>A 3</sig>

...would find [it]; whose practices are excellent, the intentions venerable, and the doctrine just, which for several centuries seems to have been destined to sift people through the **proving sieve** <term>Probsieb: literally a "test-sieve," this is a metaphorical filter used to separate the "refined" or virtuous from the "coarse" or unworthy</term>, in order to seek out from among them everywhere the good citizens, the most faithful subjects, the best fathers, tender husbands, true friends, and virtuous people. **Freemasonry** is its name; indeed a rough and insignificant designation, but we shall know how to give it weight and emphasis.

But where might it come from? How did this association arise? Who founded it? Who can maintain its existence? These are concise and urgent questions that must be answered; but first we want to reflect a little; it is sometimes good to lose oneself in the land of ideas.

Is not Adam, whom one assumes to be the father of all men, himself the most venerable being? We are all his children; but if someone wanted to prove to us their direct descent from him through a concise family tree, and present us with information and explanations—of whatever kind they may be—about it, which, I know not how, have been handed down from generation to generation and were already dictated in the Garden of Eden, or somewhere else: would one

<meta>This page continues the author's rhetorical question regarding the absurdity of tracing Freemasonry's lineage directly back to Adam in the Garden of Eden. The previous page ended with "would one", leading into the skepticism expressed here.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>
<meta>catchword: Rock,</meta>

not believe of such a person that he seeks to steal upon our sound reason in order to sell us his paradoxes as truth? Most certainly. And this is the case here; this is the cradle, the origin of the **Freemasons**, if one relies on the earliest writers—both brothers and <term>Profanes: a term used by Masons to refer to those who are not members of the order</term>—who have treated this subject. Fortunately, presumably none of them had knowledge of either the Egyptian and Chaldean systems, nor of the chronology of the Chinese; otherwise, their enthusiasm for antiquity would have driven them even further back; — so they had to content themselves with the epoch of the world's creation.

It is indeed a pity that we are only fifty-seven centuries <note>The author is referencing the traditional biblical chronology (Anno Mundi), which placed the creation of the world approximately 4,000 years before the birth of Christ. In the late 18th century, this would total about 5,700 years.</note> old, and I am almost ashamed of the great youth of our origin — yet this fable did not hold; everything contradicted it. With what should one occupy the Masons at a time when the **art of building** was still unknown, where nature—simple in both taste and needs—instilled in the human creature only small and brief concepts of those objects necessary for his survival; an overhanging

<summary>The author critiques early Masonic historians who claimed the order originated with Adam at the creation of the world, noting that such legends fail when confronted with the actual primitive state of early man.</summary>

In some of your songs, you Masons, I find this text, to which a warm imagination has given existence; it will perhaps one day prove that the **Free-weavers** or **Free-carpenters** <note>The author is using satire here, suggesting that if Masons claim antiquity because of "building," then weavers and carpenters should have even older "Free-" guilds because tents and huts preceded stone buildings.</note> are even older than you, because the use of tents—and consequently also of linen and weaving, or at least the art of braiding branches together and joining wood to construct a hut—must have existed far earlier than the art of burning lime <note>The process of heating limestone to create quicklime for mortar, a fundamental step in stone masonry.</note> and [building] a lasting—

---

*) <note>original French: "Jubal, le pere des pasteurs / Fut le premier, qui fit des tentes, / Où paisible il vivoit des rentes / De ses innocentes sueurs."</note> Jubal, the father of shepherds, / Was the first who made tents, / Where peaceful he lived on the proceeds / of his innocent sweat.

<summary>The author mocks the Masonic claim to ancient origins by pointing out that primitive humans lived in caves or tents long before stone masonry existed. He suggests that "Free-weavers" could claim even greater antiquity than Freemasons based on the biblical figure of Jubal.</summary>
<keywords>Jubal, Jabal, origins of Masonry, primitive man, tents, Free-weavers, architecture history, satire</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 7, completing the sentence regarding the invention of mortar and the impossibility of preserving Masonic records through the biblical Great Flood.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>

mortar out of it, by means of which the strongest buildings have been constructed up to now.

The Great Flood <note>German: *Sündfluth*; the biblical Deluge of Noah</note>, which put everything under water, will without doubt have washed away your yearbooks and monuments; it has been recognized that this must have occurred. A more thoughtful and reflective author has destroyed that first system <note>Referring to the "system" or theory that Masonic knowledge survived intact from the time of Adam.</note> once again. How could a single man, who escaped the general Flood, and who was busy saving so many things that would soon become very necessary to him—how could he have thought of the small designs of your minor works, of your humble sketches and tables, which must have contained the mechanics and rules of your art? And which, even then, were destined to reach posterity by means I find incomprehensible—means that you yourselves would be at a loss to prove?

No, sincerely, and for the sake—I will not say of truth, but only of probability—let us forget Adam; you are no more closely related to him than the rest of humanity; let us stick to the Patriarchs.

Noah, who was found righteous before the Lord, asked for, received, or deserved to be exempt from the general banishment; the

<summary>The author skeptically argues that any architectural records or Masonic "secrets" from the time of Adam would have been destroyed by the Great Flood, suggesting that Masons should stop claiming a direct lineage from the first man.</summary>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the speaker’s critique of Masonic history. Having previously argued that Adam could not have passed down Masonic secrets through the Great Flood, the author now examines whether Noah’s Ark constitutes a "Masonic" work.</meta>

<page-num>9</page-num>
<sig>A 5</sig>

The Creator did not wish to return the Earth to its former chaos, nor did He wish to repeat the immeasurable work of Creation: it was necessary to punish the human race, not to exterminate it, for an infinitely good Being could never desire such a thing; it was therefore also necessary to provide for its preservation and propagation.

Noah, chosen for this restoration, received instruction from the Lord regarding the means to escape the general destruction. The prescribed, measured, and variously partitioned Ark took shape and strength under his hands and through his labor, just as the Lord had commanded him; he entered it with his numerous company, which, like him, was preserved for a new repopulation of every kind: already I see him floating upon these immense floods, which will soon cover the highest mountains. Until this point, allow me, my dear brothers, I discover nothing but the **triumph of carpentry**; as yet, not a trace to the advantage of **Masonry**. <note>The author is making a pointed distinction: Noah built with wood (carpentry), not stone (masonry), suggesting that Freemasons cannot claim the Ark as a product of their specific craft.</note> Hence that invention of a newer, little-known, and moderately widespread Order, under the name of the <term>Ax Order</term> <note>original: "Axtordens" / French: "de la coignée." A reference to an obscure or perhaps satirical chivalric society associated with woodcutting or carpentry rather than stone-working.</note>, whose sign is a [axe] on a rainbow-colored 

<meta>catchword: ribbon (Bande)</meta>

<summary>The author argues that Noah’s Ark was a feat of carpentry rather than Masonry, mocking the idea that Masonic history began with the Deluge and mentioning the obscure "Ax Order."</summary>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s discussion of the "Ax Order" (de la coignée), a society he uses to contrast the craft of carpentry (associated with Noah) against the craft of Masonry. He admits to being a member of this obscure group while poking fun at the proliferation of degrees in secret societies.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>

ribbon is a hanging small golden **axe**. <note>The German OCR reads "Art" (type/kind), but the context and the previous page confirm this is a typo for "Axt" (axe), the symbol of this order.</note> It was also around this time that Noah beheld the sign of the Covenant, and the analogies are quite clear. I have the honor of belonging to this Order, of which, if I am not mistaken, there are four or five workshops <term>chantiers</term> <note>original: "chantiers." French for construction sites or lumber yards, fitting the wood-working theme of the Ax Order.</note> in France, and one in Saint-Domingue; <note>Then a French colony, now Haiti.</note> but I confess to my shame that I have almost entirely forgotten the nature of their labors. In a general sense, I recall that the whole thing consists of four degrees (for nowadays everything is "graduated"; this method is a veritable horn of plenty). These degrees are: **Apprentice**, **Journeyman**, **Perfect** or **Professed** <term>Profès</term>, and **Syrian**, whose striped sash <term>cordon</term> has seventy-two colors; but from the little I still remember, I can nonetheless affirm that of all the things the imagination has invented in modern times, this Order is the most ingenious, and its allegory is best suited to the subject. As for its purpose and intention, I shall say nothing: would one not have too much to do if one always had to give an account of the "plays of the mind," or represent an object usefully or rationally through separate images? Certainly, these gentlemen did not imagine...

<summary>The author describes his membership in the "Ax Order," an ingenious but likely satirical society with degrees like "Syrian" and "Professed," while critiquing the modern obsession with complex Masonic-style degree systems.</summary>
<keywords>Ax Order, Noah, Covenant, degrees, chantiers, Saint-Domingue, allegory, Syrian degree, cordon</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's reflections on the "Ax Order" (de la coignée). Having previously listed the degrees of the order, he now discusses the symbolic value of the Ark and critiques the modern tendency to value superficial "style" over the substance of truth.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>

to allow the plans and dimensions of the great Ark to be rescued from the ravages of time <note>original: "Zahn der Zeit" (the tooth of time).</note>: we are little worried about a new Great Flood <term>Sündfluth</term> <note>The German term specifically refers to the biblical Deluge of Noah.</note>; nowadays, so many regions of our earth, unknown back then, are familiar to us that a little corner would surely remain where one could take refuge; and in any case, physics and the art of navigation would surely offer us the means to extract ourselves from such a predicament. 

But morality gains from this fiction: the Ark is the symbol of the soul tossed upon the sea of passions; one must flee the deluge of vice. An eloquent master tells you all this, you believe it; and now, let him save himself who can! I have read somewhere that the fate of a work depends upon its style; nowadays, it is considered good form to stick merely to the shell <note>Referring to the outer appearance or surface level of a text rather than its core meaning.</note>. ## What one should think of these various <term>systems</term>. <note>In the 18th century, "systems" (Systeme) referred to the various competing branches or rites of Freemasonry, such as the Strict Observance or the Swedish Rite, each claiming a different historical origin.</note>

Worthy <term>Masonic brothers</term>! <note>maurerische Brüder</note> You who have so often had the patience to listen to these apparent impieties with edification and applause—will you have enough courage to read them? Will they not share the same fate as so many other quickly forgotten works—like those minor dramatic masterpieces that owe their value to the art and skill of the actor? If one reads them outside the dazzle of the performance, they are stale and repulsive. To evoke this feeling in you would be only a half-victory, but I desire a complete triumph. Let us speak of it rationally.

I must insist from the very start: not another word about Adam <note>The author is mocking the "Antediluvian" theory of Masonry, which claimed the craft's secrets were passed down directly from the first man, Adam.</note>. And yet, the heart is what ought to be convinced. It is easily understood that the annals of an <term>Order</term> <note>Referring to the Freemasons.</note>, which are based solely on such daring, unprovable allegations, stripped of all verified testimony, would inevitably collapse and fail to gain the slightest trust, were they not supported on the other hand by a series of analogies, practices, customs, and symbols. These all refer to the **building of the Temple in Jerusalem**, and they always lead the defenders of this allegory back to the apocryphal <note>original: "apocryphischen"; referring to stories that are not part of the accepted biblical canon or are of doubtful authenticity.</note> times of the activities surrounding this building, the detailed narrative of which has nothing but an alleged tradition <note>original: "Ueberlieferung"</note> as its guarantor. Truly, this is a poor, self-made security—one more capable of drawing a person into the most absurd errors and strangest conclusions than of clarifying the truth of the foundation, the relationship of the means, and the definition of the work.

In vain does the God-fearing philosopher consult the **holy books** <note>The Bible.</note> to rectify the cited events that are claimed to be contained therein. But they will surely admit of their own accord that we must find a more noble and definite origin for a society composed of people of every age and social standing. Is it possible to believe that after the initial surprise—which certainly leaves little room for deep thought—has passed, throughout so many years and among so many nations, not a single person has reflected, and not a single person has shared their doubts with others? Who could have barred or prevented this exchange of thoughts, which is the primary need of humanity and the first proof that we were born for a social life?

## Recent Opinion.

The mania for writing and the mania for quoting authors are so often found together, and a scholarly air is so much the tone of those whose lack of their own resources forces them to borrow, that one might lament and sigh over it. I have so much material at hand, and so little of the frenzy to appear learned, that—without detracting in the slightest from the history of the **Crusades** <note>original: "Kreuzzüge"</note>—I will quite sincerely recount word for word what is taught in the **Lodge** <note>original: "Loge"</note> on this matter. It is a story that serves as the pivot for an innumerable multitude of wheels which, unfortunately, mesh poorly with one another; this is the failing of many machines.

> "first crusades, had united under the command
> "of the pious king who led them,"

This is, in brief, the history that the **Masters of the Lodges**, those who are best informed and hold the least stock in the miraculous <note>The author suggests a preference for historical rationalism over the more fantastical or supernatural origin myths often found in 18th-century secret societies</note>, are accustomed to tell the initiate on the day of his induction with great emphasis. I have traveled much, and have been doubtful a hundred times about the path I should take; I inquired, and found a multitude of people, either ignorant or unhelpful, who, without leading me entirely astray, nonetheless distracted me more and more from the right path by pointing me toward footpaths that indeed seemed to lead closer, but which intersected at every step, bringing me backward rather than forward, and usually night fell before I reached the inn. If all **Masons** were what is called, in the division of the classes of the **Order**, **Scottish Masters of Scotland** <term>schottische Meister von Schottland: a specific high degree in 18th-century Freemasonry, particularly in the "Strict Observance" system, which claimed a direct historical line to Scottish knighthood</term>, and were consequently invested with the **Degree of Saint Andrew** <term>Grad des heiligen Andreas: a "Green" or high degree common in continental Masonic systems, often serving as a bridge between the craft degrees and the chivalric or "inner" degrees</term>—the main content of which is rational, based on facts, and supported by chronological and historical truths—it would not surprise me if one presented a newly initiated candidate with the legend of the **martyrs of the holy war** <note>This likely refers to the Crusades or the Knights Templar; many Masonic rites of this era claimed the Order was a continuation of the Templars who fell during the "holy wars"</note>.

However, so few **Masons** have reached this level of knowledge or obtained the privilege that I grant to the **Scottish Masters of Saint Andrew** <term>Schottischen Meistern vom heiligen Andreas: a high-degree rank in 18th-century Freemasonry that claimed deeper historical and philosophical knowledge than the basic degrees</term>—namely, the ability to establish as a fundamental principle what, in a certain light, might be only a secondary connection or a subordinate means. Therefore, I cannot get used to hearing a so-called **Blue Master** <term>blauer Meister: a member who has attained the third degree (Master Mason) in the basic "Blue Lodge" but has not progressed to the higher "Scottish" degrees</term>, decorated in many colors, preaching a doctrine he does not understand. This inevitably leads to what is called in good logical terms "explaining the obscure by the more obscure" <note>original: *obscurum per obscurius*—a logical fallacy where one attempts to explain a difficult concept using an even more difficult or unknown concept</note>.

I can readily admit that at the time of the **Crusades**, which I wish neither to praise nor blame here, various **Crusaders** did indeed establish a special alliance among themselves. ## Fixed Epoch.

Unceasingly rummaging through old chronicles is often the trade of pedantry <note>original: *Pedanterey*—a common 18th-century critique of dry, academic study that lacks practical application or deeper insight.</note>, and sometimes the pursuit of curiosity. To keep what has been gathered from them ever-present and in fresh memory so that one can make use of it when the occasion arises is a matter of the memory; but whoever leaves it at that has accomplished very little. Rather, to connect, distinguish, and elaborate is the work of the **understanding** <note>original: *Verstand*—referring to the analytical, logical faculty of the mind.</note>; while to judge, estimate, and decide is the triumph of **reason** <note>original: *Vernunft*—in Enlightenment philosophy, this is the highest faculty, allowing for moral and universal judgment.</note>.

The oldest warriors, the first who possessed the form of a disciplined corps, were the <term>Knights of the Dawn</term> <note>original: *Ritter von der Morgenröthe*—likely a reference to early chivalric or mystical orders claimed as precursors to Freemasonry.</note> and of the <term>Promised Land</term>. These were the ancestors and fathers of the <term>Masons</term> <note>original: *Maurer*</note>—those enlightened and famous men, the timing of whose origin I shall not state, nor shall I betray their secret. They had all watched with sorrow the tragic changes that the Kingdom of Judah had suffered over time, and had long hoped that God would finally deign to cast a gracious eye upon this Holy Land, where His presence had already been revealed since the first <term>legislation</term> <note>original: *Gesetzgebung*—this refers to the Giving of the Law (the Torah) to Moses on Mount Sinai.</note>. It was still unknown to most,

### Order, Royal Art, Lodge.

**The** plow of Camillus and the spade of Curius <note>Marcus Furius Camillus and Manius Curius Dentatus were legendary Roman heroes celebrated for their extreme humility and for returning to their simple farms after achieving great military victories.</note> (see *The Pharsalia*, translated by Marmontel <note>Jean-François Marmontel, a French historian and writer, published a well-known translation of Lucan in 1766.</note>) have produced more heroes and great men than have ever sprung from the most famous lineages. Great events spring from the smallest causes. Grand expense, luster, and splendor are often the grave of virtue; poverty is generally the mother of noble deeds. Sometimes, too, "poverties" or trivialities <note>The author is making a pun on the German words "Armuth" (poverty) and "Armseligkeiten" (trivialities or wretchedness).</note> (if a pun may be permitted) produce wonderful phantasms: great words do not always signify great things. Are there many titles firmly enough established to withstand a test of investigation? My first doubt rests upon the name commonly given to Masonry: the **Order of Freemasons**. Let us pose a dilemma; either one confesses...

<meta>The previous page ended with "either one confesses...", beginning a dilemma regarding the title "Order." ...the public granted our brothers this illustrious designation—which, strictly speaking, would imply a great deal—of their own accord, then it would be more a case of misuse than correct usage. Or, if the Masons <term>Maurer<gloss>Masons; in this context, the Freemasons</gloss></term> have usurped this title for themselves—"possession is valid" <note>original: "possessio valet"; a Latin legal maxim suggesting that current possession of a title or property is presumed valid until proven otherwise</note>, as the laws say—they rely upon this, and custom gains the upper hand. In the first case, the brothers were in the wrong, and in the second, they are also not right.

What is an Order? Let us use common concepts and a simple answer, without bombastic expressions. An Order is a specific society whose origin is known, whose actions are public, whose regulations are established, whose purpose is determined, and whose utility is demonstrated. Its authority rests directly upon the protection of the Sovereign, upon diplomas of confirmation, or upon explicit agreements made between princes to mutually approve such institutions and special arrangements under a specific name, on certain conditions, and for a particular end goal, and to grant it a degree of respect by which the public is meant to be guided. I would not know how else an Order might be defined.

Since the Temple of Solomon is always the focal point for the Masons, from whatever perspective one views them, it is to be presumed that they adopted the name Lodge <note>original: *Loge*</note> in reference to that very Temple. Perhaps the Freemasons also borrowed this expression from the Italian: *alloggio* <note>original: *Allogio*</note> means as much as "lodging" or "dwelling," because the meetings were undoubtedly held in the house of the elected leader who presided over them. <meta>This page continues the discussion of Masonic organizational structure. The catchword from the previous page "Pror" and the starting "gen" of this page form the word "Prorogationen" (prorogations), referring here to the extension of authority or the formal establishment of assemblies.</meta>

...[taking place as proroga]tions <note>original: *Prorogationen*. In this context, it refers to the formal act of extending sessions or the establishment of new branches under existing authority.</note> according to the same rules, for the same purpose, and following the same principles, which may have been established—and can still be established—through the mere gathering of various good and genuine brothers. These brothers, being essentially free and without detracting from the central leadership, require nothing more for such an undertaking than their own free will; unless, however, they have agreed among themselves to recognize a Superior, <term>Primas</term> <note>A title for a high-ranking presiding officer, often used in a regional or national context within certain orders.</note>, <term>Grand Master</term>, or a Supreme <term>Tribunal</term> in this or that province, to which they have voluntarily submitted themselves.

In such a case, it becomes a matter of specific discipline and <term>internal regulation</term> <note>original: *Polizeysache*. In the 18th century, this referred to the general administration and "policy" that maintained order within a community, rather than modern criminal policing.</note>, which they may no longer arbitrarily evade. Nor could they do so without overturning the rules once they have been accepted, for the precise maintenance of these rules has a great influence on the duration, the strength, and the validity of all ordinances. It is an old maxim: *He who fails by a syllable, fails by the whole* <note>original: *qui cadit a syllaba cadit a toto*. A legal maxim suggesting that a small error in form or procedure can invalidate an entire legal action.</note>, (he who errs in the small, errs also in the great): one must adhere most strictly to agreements once they have been reached, when the fate of a society depends on their good or poor observance.

# The Profane:
### what they imagine regarding the final purpose of Masonry: various Masons' conceptions thereof.

"The weeds, unfortunately, grow just as much in the fields of Jerusalem as they do in the fields of Samaria;" <note>This is a biblical allegory. Jerusalem represents the "true" or "sacred" center, while Samaria was often viewed as its schismatic or corrupted rival. The speaker suggests that flaws and bad actors exist even within the most sacred institutions.</note> so once expressed himself a virtuous Mason whose office required him to enlighten and instruct others. This man, who possessed not only the advantage of a very good heart but also the finest mind and the most beautiful eloquence, thus described with a single stroke all the disruptions that Masonry has suffered for several years now, and the profanations which degrade it. His statement struck me; I applied it further. Not all of those who have not been initiated into the secrets of Masonry are, for that reason, exclusively **profane**: <note>In Masonic terms, "profane" (from the Latin *pro fano*, "before or outside the temple") refers to anyone not initiated into the craft. It is not necessarily an insult, though the author here notes its offensive potential.</note> nevertheless, the Society daily labels them with this offensive epithet: *I hate the uninitiated crowd and keep them away.* <note>original Latin: *Odi profanum vulgus, & arceo*. A famous line from the Roman poet Horace (Odes 3.1.1), often used to express elitism or the guarding of sacred mysteries.</note> Was Horace perhaps a Freemason? He hates the profane rabble and keeps them at a distance. *Vulgus* <note>Latin: the common people or the masses.</note> can be translated as the people, the rabble,

the great crowd, common people, or perhaps translate it with something even worse; <note>The author is continuing the translation of Horace’s *profanum vulgus* from the previous page, exploring how "the profane" can be rendered as "the masses" or "the rabble."</note> all of this aligns with the tone in which the **Freemasons** <term>Freemasons: Members of a fraternal organization that grew out of the local guilds of stonemasons, known for their use of secret rituals and symbols.</term> commonly speak of all those who are not among their number. Every nation, every sect, every society that forms a so-called special bond—which hates, despises, or fears everything that does not belong to it—must have a specific term to designate those who are foreign to it. Among the English, anyone who does not—as they do—drink punch like a madman, wear short wigs, possess a gloomy head, a bad mood, and coarse manners, is called a *French Dog*. <note>original English in text: "French Dog." A common 18th-century English slur for foreigners, particularly the French, reflecting the intense national rivalry of the era.</note> This otherwise venerable nation, despite the fact that it imitates the ridiculous and forced behaviors of its neighbors in every respect, harbors such a general, heartfelt loathing for them that it knows no better way to express its contempt for all those not born in London or in the Kingdom than by calling every foreigner "French," or a "Frenchman." In the most sacred books, generally all those who are not Jews are called **Heathens**, <note>original: *Heiden*. Even nowadays in **Rome**, anyone must be called a **heretic** <term>heretic: Someone whose beliefs go against official church doctrine.</term> who does not kiss the Holy Father's slipper, and yet there is certainly no great mystery hidden beneath it. <note>The "Holy Father's slipper" refers to the traditional practice of kissing the Pope's foot as a sign of respect and submission.</note>

The mysteries of **Osiris** in **Egypt**, the **Eleusinians** in Greece, the **Good Goddess** in Rome, and so many others of this kind—with which the history of every century and all nations teems—had a somewhat serious appearance. No one who was not initiated was allowed to approach the walls; *stay far away, you uninitiated* <note>original Latin: *procul estote profani*. A ritual formula used in ancient Roman religion to warn the unpurified or uninitiated to keep their distance from sacred rites.</note>; thus, everyone was known from whom participation in the secret **orgies** <term>orgies: In the ancient context, "orgia" referred to the secret rites or festivals of mystery religions, particularly those of Dionysus or Demeter, and did not necessarily carry the modern connotation of debauchery.</term> and **ceremonies** was forbidden. Do your secrets have any resemblance to those? Why then such a similar anxious caution, if the **symbols** are so little alike? Leave the explanation of this to me; you shall lose nothing by it. Every person who applies himself to useful discoveries, or at least to those he considers as such,

**Casuists** <note>Those who study "cases of conscience," typically theologians or lawyers who resolve moral dilemmas through specific rules.</note>, historians, artists, etc.—they are likely always the same kind of people, but they do not share the same point of view. Those who treat us most leniently believe our only purpose is merely pleasant **pastime**, and the whole **secret** consists in making others believe that one actually possesses a secret. The glutton judges us by the delicacy of our meals and the reputation of the caterer; the drunkard by the small excesses in wine which, despite the strictness of the laws, cannot be entirely prevented; the kind-hearted man by a few well-timed **alms**; each judges in his own way. The devout man judges by a solemn **Mass** that has been sung with splendor in this or that church; only recently has it been forbidden to us in certain **parishes** to pray for the deceased—oh! on this subject I have a whole collection of beautiful, malicious, and annoying anecdotes, which are the only ones of their kind! > *) This description is not very favorable to you; nevertheless, I love you to the extreme, despite your faults. <note>Original German: "Diese Schilderung ist euch nicht allzu vortheilhaft; dennoch lieb ich euch, eurer Fehler ohngeachtet aufs äußerste."</note>

Yes, my brothers, I love you, and indeed with all my heart, and for that very reason I want to quarrel with you a little: why do you expose yourselves so much? "Show me your faith through your works" is a proverb that applies to everything; either show fully who you are, and triumph will be certain for you; or let nothing else be seen but real, active **Masonic virtue** <term>Maurertugend: the specific moral qualities—such as brotherly love, relief, and truth—that Masons are expected to practice.</term>. I know very well that according to your fundamental laws and rules, your **architecture** <note>Original: "Baukunst." <page-num>75</page-num>

...searched through societies existing since the **creation of the world**, which proves so clearly and distinctly that at all times and everywhere only men have preached to men, and indeed without success, fruitlessly, and often even to the detriment of **reason** and **humanity**. It must have escaped him that a society—existing perhaps even before the **Crusades**, but certainly long before the **war of the Waldensians** <note>A late-medieval Christian movement that sought to return to apostolic poverty; they were declared heretical and suffered significant persecution.</note> and the **fury of the League** <note>original: "Ligue"; referring to the Catholic League of France, a major faction during the French Wars of Religion in the 16th century.</note>—has held the exclusive privilege of delivering immeasurable sermons that never end, which are necessarily and frequently repeated, yet do not weary the listener. Instead, they are heard gladly; they convince, improve, purify the heart, and are extraordinarily edifying and useful. Can this be understood? Certainly not.

The **moral purpose** is not, however, the primary concern of the **Freemasons**; it is, at most, an excellent secondary matter. If used properly, it can sometimes pleasantly occupy the mind, perhaps even touch the heart a little, and always satisfy the passion and vanity of the **orator** <term>Redner: In a Masonic context, this refers to the Brother Orator, an officer responsible for delivering lectures on the history, laws, and ethics of the craft.</term> who practices it. <meta>This page continues the speaker's critique of contemporary Freemasonry, shifting from the motivations of leaders to the confusion of the rank-and-file members. Have they been able to occupy themselves with this for six to seven centuries to achieve such uncertain and incoherent aims? No. The **Masons** <note>original: "Maurer"</note> are collectively deceiving themselves; they do not know the origin and fail to recognize the goal. What is the goal?

O you who ask me, are you worthy of me revealing it to you? If I could forget my **duty** <note>original: "Pflicht"</note>, violate my honor, and betray my **secret** <note>original: "Geheimnis"</note> and my brothers—which I shall never do—would you even deserve that I do so for your sake? <meta>This page continues the author's exploration of secret or misunderstood societies. Having just mentioned "Aesop's rooster"—who in the fable finds a pearl but prefers a grain of corn—the author titles this section "Sacred Pearls." He compares the Moravian Brethren to the Freemasons, suggesting both are largely harmless, law-abiding groups despite the rumors or scandals that occasionally surround them.</meta>

# Sacred Pearls. Misuse of Expressions. Veneration of Numbers.

Near Utrecht, a small piece of land serves as a residence for a sect called the **Moravian Brethren** <note>original: "Herrnhuter," a Pietist Christian denomination founded by Count Zinzendorf</note>. Their leader once made himself invisible by disappearing with the society's treasury. For a long time, people argued about the nature of the connection, the doctrine, the customs, the morals, the way of life, the maintenance, and the intentions of this handful of people. But these honest, quiet people followed the laws, paid their taxes to the state, were a burden to no one, worked together, lived in community, and dreamed as they pleased; they were forgotten, and their association still continues. 

In every city, on certain days, some twenty or so **Freemasons** <note>original: "Freymäurer"</note> are gathered in a small room; their treasury sometimes exists only in their imagination, and often the Master <note>The leader of a Masonic Lodge</note> appropriates the funds for himself. For a considerable time, people have occupied themselves with the bond that unites them, with their customs, their means, their intentions, and their amusements; but these honest people, who enjoy themselves in a decent manner at their festivals, obey the laws,

<meta>This page continues from the author's previous reflections on the duty of a "citizen of the world" to observe and report on the customs of different groups. Skill and fluency in languages is not granted to everyone, nor can it always be forced through diligence. Nature has organized certain minds in such a way that they absorb the impression of foreign and diverse words with little effort, and can retain them in memory without exertion; even though this has its clear benefits, it is fundamentally only a very minor merit, with which—as I can reliably assert—one will not exactly make a great fortune.

It is indisputably a great pleasure to know different languages; one is then a stranger nowhere. But I appeal to those who possess this charming talent (for to always speak of oneself is ridiculous and foolish): does not every nation, besides the general vernacular, have a particular usage—the so-called **good tone** <term>guten Ton: Refers to the refined social etiquette and specific vocabulary used by the upper classes to distinguish themselves from the common people.</term>—which consists of certain established words from which one cannot deviate without appearing vulgar <note>original: "pöbelhaft"</note> and common? I know a country where pride in titles constitutes the national character, so much so that people, who are divided into the first, second, and third classes, dare not have social contact with anyone outside their rank without degrading themselves. In truth, the **Masons**, taken in the general sense and according to what they usually do, do not deal with particularly serious or important matters; their manner of proceeding is thus a real misuse in this regard. <meta>Continuing from the previous page's critique of Masonic terminology, such as the use of "it is raining" to signal the presence of outsiders, the author now transitions to the corruption of ritual passwords and the absurdity of higher-degree ceremonies.</meta>

Among the passwords commonly used in **Masonic circles** there are some that are not Hebrew: in a certain case, one cannot enter the **Lodge** without whispering into someone’s ear: "so-and-so was a good **Mason**," even if during a previous circumstance it was asserted that this very person was a scoundrel, a faithless man. There is a certain very exalted dignity <note>This likely refers to the "Elu" or "Elect" degrees in 18th-century Freemasonry, which often focused on the themes of mourning and justice for the murdered Master of the Temple.</note>, distinguished from the others by embroidery and ribbons; when the brothers of this class are assembled, one cannot enter the meeting place without first having wept at the door and having piteously told the one who opens the door that **the cat is dead** <note>original: *que le Chat est mort*</note>: I say **cat** <note>original: *le Chat*</note> to make the likely misunderstanding all the more apparent, for in fact it means **the Shah** <note>original: *der Schach*</note>, an Oriental expression which signifies **Lord** or **Regent**, but which, by passing from mouth to mouth and being written in a hundred different ways, was finally so distorted; fundamentally it comes to the same thing and is not worth the investigation. --- Page 113 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, the author finishes the thought that while one shouldn't blame everyone for the mistakes of an individual, the systemic issues within Freemasonry belong to the whole group.</meta>

...errors <note>Completing the word "Fehler" from the previous page.</note> of an individual should not be blamed on everyone at once; I gladly admit this. However, the flaws spread across the entire mass—those inherent defects that arise from the matter itself—are common to all. To possess "sacred words" is a minor folly that may be excused; to misuse these expressions is a deception, and one that cannot be disguised. To involve oneself extensively with numbers, seeking something sublime and wonderful within them, might perhaps not be the greatest folly, if only the reverence for them stemmed from better reasoning.

Among the **Freemasons**, everything happens by **three**: three brothers constitute a regular **Lodge**, three principal officers govern it, three lights illuminate it, three exquisite jewels adorn it, three essential pieces comprise its implements <note>In Masonic tradition, these "three essential pieces" usually refer to the "Furniture of the Lodge": the Holy Bible, the Square, and the Compasses.</note>, through three knocks they signal the order for command or dissolve it, three specific questions define the character of the **Mason**, three steps compose the gait, three actual degrees indeed contain the essence of all **Masonry**, and three years constitute the full age of an **initiate** in the first class <note>This refers to the symbolic age of an Entered Apprentice; in ritual, when asked how old they are, a member of the first degree traditionally answers "three years."</note>. This precise adherence to the **triple number** <note>original: *gedritten Zahl*</note> promises at first glance a—

->Prohibition against writing. Oath. Secrecy.<-
->Lodge banquet. Brothers.<-

It is indeed a vexing circumstance, the causes of which I would very much like to know, that in all the reflections I occasionally make regarding Masonry <term>Maurerey</term>, nothing but unpleasant axioms <note>self-evident truths or established principles</note> occur to me, which have an all-too-critical appearance. "Too much precaution is a deception" <note>original: *Nimia præcautio dolus*; a Latin legal maxim suggesting that excessive secrecy often masks a fraud</note>, for example, is an old principle which the prohibition against writing—so strictly enforced among the Freemasons—brings back to my memory against my will. Am I then truly resentful toward a society with which I have voluntarily joined, or does it in fact itself give cause for censure and reproof? I wish to settle this account with myself.

It is certain that, far from harboring the slightest bitterness <note>original: *Galle*, literally "gall" or "bile"</note> against my brothers, I have instead a thousand reasons to boast of their good conduct toward me: the behavior of a few individuals toward me, who perhaps were in the wrong—

<meta>catchword: ben (completing the word "haben" or "have")</meta>

<summary>The author reflects on his internal conflict regarding Freemasonry, questioning why the organization's strict rules of secrecy and prohibitions against writing trigger his suspicion despite his positive personal experiences with its members.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, secrecy, Latin maxims, skepticism, fraternal conduct, oath of silence</keywords>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's defense of his writing. He transitions from discussing personal slights to his broader philosophical mission: justifying his decision to publish details about Freemasonry despite the order's strict rules of secrecy.</meta>

might have been in the wrong—the ingratitude of certain automata <note>referring to people who act mechanically or without independent thought</note> which I could well have expected, the falsehood of some whom I have served and who have betrayed me, the arrogance of some, the pride of others, the social tone, the deceit, the flaws of education, the stubbornness, and the small annoyances that occasionally grew from these—all of this affected me for a few moments at most. It will never influence my good disposition <term>Gesinnung</term> toward society in general, a fact I believe I am most clearly substantiating through this small philosophical dissertation, written both for and against it.

But—have I not perhaps wished to justify myself because of the free manner in which I now write, and with which I examine the work and the science of the Masons <term>Maurer</term>, as I endeavor to show that they are wrong to forbid all written, painted, drawn, or engraved accounts of their customs? No, this was not my intention; I believe the motive that inspires me to this undertaking justifies me sufficiently: I have wanted to lead the public out of error, to enlighten my brothers, to perhaps improve their abuses, and to show their excellent qualities.

<summary>The author argues that his personal disappointments with certain individuals do not diminish his overall love for society, and he justifies his decision to write openly about Masonic secrets as a means of enlightening both the public and his fellow brothers.</summary>

I place the Masons <term>Maurer</term> at their dressing table <term>Putztisch</term> <note>a vanity or grooming table used for dressing and applying cosmetics</note> to adorn them with their true beauties; would it please them better if I used nothing but makeup <term>Schminke</term> and beauty marks <term>Schönfleckchen</term> <note>small, often heart-shaped patches of black silk or velvet applied to the face, popular in 18th-century fashion to highlight pale skin</note> for the task?

They can feel nothing but gratitude toward me for this undertaking, and thus I would not know why I should justify myself in advance, as I have no secret or wicked intentions: if I therefore make bitter remarks, the fault lies with Masonry <term>Maurerey</term> itself; it is the craft itself that leads me to them.

### Concerning the Degrees. The Absurdity of Some Degrees: The Uselessness of Almost All.

Famous letter **G** <note>In Freemasonry, the letter 'G' typically stands for Geometry or God (the Grand Architect of the Universe). It is a central symbol in many rituals.</note>, whose dark imprint I constantly see occupying the center of the five-pointed star <note>Known as the 'Blazing Star,' a prominent symbol in the Second Degree of Freemasonry.</note> which shines in the Temple of the Masons, and whose artificial light at times illuminates their work—how have you come to be the indicator of their frosty symbols? The alphabet which they have fashioned for themselves is suited to their strange way of thinking and expresses no truth; it is of a mystical character, much like a chameleon, which from one moment to the next takes on the colors held before it. But every possible **G** in the world, combined in a hundred different ways, cannot make fables true, justify assertions, or confirm chimerical <note>Something imagined or illusory, like a dream that cannot come true.</note> rights and erroneous systems. The flaming star, which serves as a border for all these allegories, will

It remains to be seen whether Nature would not have done better to leave men in the original state in which she placed them, rather than—as happened afterward—arranging them into distinct and separate classes. Whatever origin that small society <note>Referring to the Freemasons, particularly the specific branches or "high degrees" discussed on the previous page.</note> may have—to which people have done the honor of attributing very deep, very widespread intentions, and which some people have even suspected of harboring the great project of a **universal republic**; a wretched system, yet trimmed with everything methodical and seductive that the spirit of **anarchy** possesses, and whose invention is attributed to **Cromwell** <note>original: "Cromwell." I will not, however, dwell on the refutation of this entirely improbable opinion, which fundamentally contradicts all Masonic obligations and vows; but in order to judge this society properly, one must at least for the time being accept the **hypothesis** put forward by its members regarding the moment of its founding. 

The **geometer** <term>geometer: a mathematician; in a Masonic context, geometry is seen as the foundational science of the universe and a symbol of moral order</term> establishes a point; this specific point extends into infinity and, through its path, produces the multitude of lines from which he constructs his **triangles** and **squares**: for lack of mathematical truths, let us assume something that is accepted as truth by convention and work accordingly.

<meta>This page transitions from the author's dismissal of political conspiracy theories (like those involving Cromwell) to a direct critique of the Masonic degree system. The author now challenges the "science" of the order by looking at its central allegory: the Temple of Solomon.</meta>

The construction of the **Temple in Jerusalem**, its destruction, and its rebuilding—this, my brothers, if I am not mistaken, is the ultimate goal of your association, your science, and your study. <note>The Temple of Solomon is the central architectural allegory of Freemasonry, representing both the individual's moral growth and the structure of a perfect society.</note> Answer me! Could a society that came together according to such a plan, and for such purposes, have at any time allowed foreign matters to be mixed into its business? Matters that do not rhyme with the former at all, and which at every step tear apart the chain of events and the plan of both previous and future operations?

The **Freemasons** agreed—regardless of when, how, and why—upon a certain symbolic form for the initiation of those they wished to admit into their ranks. The degrees of **Apprentice** <term>Apprentice: (German: Lehrling) the first level of Masonic membership, symbolizing a beginner in the craft of moral self-improvement</term> and **Fellow Craft** <term>Fellow Craft: (German: Geselle) the second degree, traditionally associated with the mastery of professional skills and the study of the liberal arts</term>, which are nearly identical, were, in my opinion, sufficient to cast a veil of mystery over the first proceedings. These had to be made difficult for fear that one would too soon perceive the emptiness of what follows: if one goes beyond the first two classes—which any man free of delusion would gladly combine into a single one—what can compare to the immeasurable series of degrees derived from them?

<summary>The author critiques the proliferation of Masonic degrees, arguing that the essential lessons of the "Temple of Jerusalem" allegory are obscured by unnecessary and confusing additions. He suggests the initial degrees are made artificially difficult to hide the lack of substance in higher levels.</summary>

--- Page 148 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's scathing critique of the Masonic degree system. Having previously argued that the first two degrees were enough to hide the "emptiness" of the organization, the author now pivots to a broader philosophical attack on the very concept of social and fraternal "degrees" or ranks.</meta>

Everything has its limits, except for the **obstinacy** of the human spirit; just as boundless in its whims as in its desires, it will fashion a new level of **science** <note>In this context, the author uses "science" (Wissenschaft) ironically to describe the complex, pseudo-intellectual systems of Masonic degrees.</note> out of every means it deems suitable to expand its domain or to increase the tribute it exacts from fools, all to raise the throne of **folly** higher and higher; this indecent expansionism <note>original: "Plusmacherey," a derogatory 18th-century term for artificial value-creation or profiteering.</note> will have no end. 

What, fundamentally, is a **degree**, if one takes this word in its naked, true meaning and in relation to all walks of life? Is it not the arbitrary measure dictated by authority to determine the apparent merit of every person? This is done without allowing any contrary conviction to complain about the shackles placed upon it, nor allowing one to withhold the reverence which this **despotic tariff** <note>A "tariff" here refers to a fixed scale of social or moral worth imposed by an organization.</note> demands and claims for itself. 

Every social class experiences this wondrous **gradation** <term>Gradation: (German: Abstufung) the hierarchical ranking or "ladder" within a social or fraternal system</term>, which separates people from one another even when they are equal. It often gives the **good-for-nothing** <term>Good-for-nothing: (German: Taugenichts) a person of no merit or use to society</term> the unjust claim to be noticed more—though not to be worth more—than the clever, honest, and virtuous man.

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of hierarchical systems, transitioning from a general condemnation of social ranks to a specific analysis of the Masonic degree system. The first **painter** who sketched the portrait of **social ranks** and **dignities** certainly ground his colors on the palette of **delusion**; flattery provided the shading; the brush was bold because the painter was servile, perhaps needy, and most certainly base. Let us draw the curtain over these shameful images; they offend the truth too much. 

We wish to direct our gaze toward other objects. The **Masons** <term>Masons: (German: Maurer) members of the Freemasonic brotherhood</term> present us with quite a variety, yet it is always the same sight; their **degrees** <term>Degrees: (German: Grade) the hierarchical steps or ranks within the lodge</term> fall back into the flawed circle whose outline we have just drawn. For even if it were possible to grant them the likelihood of a **mastery** <term>Mastery: (German: Meisterschaft) referring to the third degree of "Master Mason"</term> beyond that of the **Apprentice** <term>Apprentice: (German: Lehrling) the introductory degree</term> and **Journeyman** <term>Journeyman: (German: Geselle) the second degree</term>—because in a society where one presumes a school exists, one must also necessarily presume more learned and enlightened masters who impart knowledge—what will follow from this? 

Let us even concede, if one wishes, to avoid all bickering, a higher class which I will allow the Masons: draftsmen, **architects**, **contractors** <note>original: "Unternehmer"; in this 18th-century context, this refers to those who "undertake" or manage large building projects, continuing the architectural metaphor of the craft.</note>, in short, whatever they please to call them, provided they attach some meaning to the name; and that is surely to concede everything most generously.

### I 3

...to assist each other mutually, which prompted the **contributions** and the **fees** that were imposed on the **candidates**; from the proceeds of these receipts, public capital was to be pooled together for the **society** <note>original: "Gesellschaft"; referring to the Masonic lodge or brotherhood.</note>. These **funds** became a **resource** <note>original: "Hülfsquelle"</note> that truly benefited brothers who were unfortunate and worthy of assistance. Others, enticed by a **benevolence** <note>original: "Wohlthätigkeit"</note> that flowed only from magnanimous compassion, believed they had the same claims to the common **treasury**; greedy for the advantage of drawing from it, they made demands. Since they deserved nothing, they were turned away. 

The bitter memory of this supposed injustice inspired them with the plan to appropriate for themselves a certain portion of the contributions to the treasury, without rendering an account of their office to anyone—just as a **chief treasurer** might dispose of the funds entrusted to him at will, without any of the subordinate officials being allowed to call him to account. 

In order to arrive at this hateful **extortion** <term>extortion: the original term "Geldschneiderey" implies "money-cutting" or profiteering through trickery.</term>, one had to impose new **taxes**, create objects of appraisal, pretend an authorization, and assume a superior right. Hateful **cunning**, we wish to pair you with lowly **avarice** <note>original: "Geiz"</note>, and you shall produce **degrees** <note>original: "Grade"; the author is cynically suggesting that higher Masonic degrees were invented merely to create new fees and hierarchical control.</note>: a ridiculous childbed, which

<summary>The author argues that the invention of higher Masonic degrees and complex hierarchies was not a spiritual evolution, but a result of greed and the desire to embezzle communal funds without oversight.</summary>

### 149

...[whose] health and pleasure shine from his eyes, comfortably ensconced in a pleasant carriage, whom I see driving politely around the promenades and fine houses with the air of luxury and idleness; and I ask who he is, and they answer me: a **canon** <term>Domherr: A member of a cathedral chapter. In the 18th century, these positions were often held by aristocrats and were associated with significant wealth and social prestige.</term>, a **prebendary** <term>Pfründner: A cleric who receives a "prebend"—a portion of the revenues from a cathedral or collegiate church—often without having many specific duties.</term>; that is enough for me. I know my man; I do not inquire whether he is a **provost** <term>Probst: The head of a cathedral chapter or a high-ranking administrative cleric.</term>, or the first or second **dean** <term>Dechant: A senior official within a church chapter.</term> of the chapter, nor what honorary positions are attached to his **prebend** <term>Pfründe: The income or estate attached to a church office.</term> or his dignity.

Not everything superfluous is corrupting, but everything that is merely incidental—everything that entangles the subject with unnecessary circumstances which serve more to degrade than to elevate it—is a fault that must be remedied. Such things bring shame to **reason** <term>Vernunft: In the Enlightenment context, this refers to the capacity for rational thought and moral judgment.</term>, do harm to the **spirit** <term>Geist: Often translated as mind or spirit; the intellectual and moral essence of a person.</term>, and corrupt the **heart**.

### 150

### Morality, Jurisdiction, Administration <note>original: "Policey" — in the 18th century, this term referred to the general internal administration, order, and welfare of a state, far broader than the modern "police."</note>.

"Nothing more than that?" my publisher said to me the day before yesterday: "Truly, my dear sir, if I knew that the second volume was worth no more than the first, I would not dare to concern myself with the work; what interest do you expect the public to take in it? At first, it seems as if you want to reveal all the secrets, but at the bottom, one finds nothing but over-rationalizing: I know nothing more frigid, nothing more tasteless in the world, than **rationalizing** <term>Raisonnements: From the French 'raisonnement,' referring here to abstract, often tedious arguments or intellectualizing.</term>; nothing is more arbitrary; everyone indulges in it, every thinking being has the right to prattle on, everyone meddles in it, but what does one learn from it? Pretty assumptions—well, who doesn't make those?"

A **reasoner** <term>Vernünftler: A derogatory Enlightenment-era term for someone who over-thinks or engages in pedantic, superficial rationalization.</term> is a burdensome person; one scribbles every day according to one's own ideas, but because of that, not a single one is firmly established for the reader. — I waited every moment for him to direct Boileau's <note>Nicolas Boileau-Despréaux (1636–1711) was an influential French poet and critic known for his insistence on reason and classical standards in literature.</note> apostrophe at me:

For my part, I do not wish to risk my effort, time, and expense by printing a rag whose foundation seems so shaky to me, and whose sales appear so doubtful. The part where you promised speeches, I will still allow; if they are well-crafted, people may perhaps find pleasure in them. The public will be curious about them; the **Masons** <term>Maurer: Freemasons; the 18th century saw a massive boom in Masonic lodges, and there was a high demand for published ritual speeches and "secret" knowledge.</term>, who have speeches to deliver, will take refuge there in their time of need. And that **Adept degree** <term>Adeptengrad: A high rank in a secret society or alchemical order, denoting someone who has reached a level of "mastery" or enlightenment.</term>, which presents a kind of philosophical treatise on the **great work** <term>das große Werk: Traditionally the alchemical *Magnum Opus* (the creation of the Philosopher's Stone), but here likely referring to the ultimate spiritual or social goals of a secret society.</term>, will likely also have some merit. As for everything else, believe me, there is much to trim, much to purge, much to polish, and in the end, all that will come of it is a clutter of words that signifies nothing.

The clever talk of a man who deals with us and seeks to profit from our circumstances—how hard that is to hear! I stood as if on hot coals, but what work does not have its frustrations? The frustration of **authorship** is the worst of all: the applause and cheers of the public are the roses of this trade; the disputes, the negotiations, the initial criticism from the **bookseller** <term>Buchhändler: In this era, booksellers often functioned as publishers, editors, and distributors all in one.</term> are terrible thorns: one pricks oneself on them, one must

**Masonry** <term>Maurerey: Freemasonry; a fraternal organization that uses the tools and language of stonemasonry as allegories for moral and spiritual growth.</term> is an optical painting <note>The author is likely describing an "anamorphic" painting, a popular 18th-century artistic curiosity where a distorted image only becomes recognizable when viewed from a specific angle or through a special mirror.</note> which, if it is to reveal its true image, must be viewed from a fixed vantage point, though it can otherwise be looked at from all sides. ...about which clever people almost no longer wish to speak; a rosy-cheeked, robust abbot brought up the subject of Masonry <note>original: "Maurerey"</note>; everyone had their say. Two or three women condemned us instantly; a complaint from their sex arose, claiming that one cannot value or justify men who amuse themselves among themselves and allow no women <note>original: "Frauenzimmer"; in the 18th century, this was a standard term for women, though it later acquired a more dismissive tone.</note> into their meetings. 

A little creature with red heels <note>Red-heeled shoes were a distinct status symbol of the 18th-century aristocracy and "fops," often associated with vanity and French court fashion.</note>, dressed up like a doll and smelling of ambergris <note>original: "Ambra"; a rare substance used in high-end perfumes of the era, known for its powerful, musky scent.</note> enough to make one faint, was indignant at the "filthiness" of an order that worked and conducted its business only by burning candles, in whose heat the fellow brothers—who were forced to sit always serious, always stiff, and squeezed close together in the haze of the candles—nearly perished. 

Suddenly, a cracked voice rang out from a corner of the room; I turned around and beheld a large wig from under which it emanated. It began to present the "mighty proof" of our godlessness, which could clearly be seen, it claimed, from our oath and from the mixture of religions <note>A common 18th-century criticism of Freemasonry was its "latitudinarianism"—the fact that it accepted members of different Christian denominations and even different faiths, which was seen by some as a threat to religious orthodoxy.</note> found within the order. 

The speaker grew heated; I had remained silent until then, but I ventured to answer and assured them that the society generally did nothing that was contrary to religion, or to the loyalty one owes to the princes, to the state, or to one's

> "Do not be too hasty," said Imlac to Rasselas, "to give your trust or your admiration to the teachers of morality; they speak like heavenly spirits, but they live like men." <note>This quote is from Samuel Johnson’s 1759 novella *The History of Rasselas, Prince of Abissinia*, which was widely translated and popular in 18th-century Europe as a meditation on the human search for happiness.</note>

Indeed, if spiritual societies <note>original: "geistliche Gesellschaften"; here referring to religious orders or monastic communities that live by a specific rule.</note>, which hold together under the various banners <term>Panieren: Derived from the French "bannières," referring to the standards or flags of a founder or a military/religious leader.</term> of their founders and distinguish themselves by the variations of their liveries <term>Libereyen: Distinctive clothing or habits worn by members of a specific order or household.</term>, are secure places of refuge for virtue and impenetrable sanctuaries <note>original: "Freystätte"; historically a legal "city of refuge" or asylum where a person was safe from secular prosecution.</note> against vice, they are so because they combine the theory of religious duties <term>Religionspflichten: The specific moral and ritual obligations required by a person's faith.</term> with the devout and daily practice of those same duties. This explanation and exception is the most favorable that can be made on behalf of monasteries and celibate orders: my brothers <note>Referring to fellow Freemasons.</note> certainly have an advantage in this regard; they contribute to the great whole, each for his part, and they know how to combine their obligations toward the general public with their special duties: nothing in this arrangement stands in contrast.

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 175 regarding the definition of a true citizen. It picks up mid-sentence from the previous page's thought about the rarity of genuine civic virtue.</meta>

...would wish that such people had always been rare, for one does not make much of a fuss about a virtue or an action when it is commonplace. If one were to instill the idea of the common interest <term>allgemeinen Interesses: The "common good" or collective welfare of society, a central concept in Enlightenment political philosophy.</term>—which always arrives too late and never of its own accord—before the idea of personal interest, which always arrives too early and entirely alone, yet to which the former is subordinated and connected by invisible bonds; then self-interest <term>Eigennutz: Selfishness or the pursuit of private gain at the expense of the community.</term> would speak less loudly, and the fatherland would find itself improved.

The Burgher of Calais <note>original: "Bürger von Calais"; a reference to the six citizens who offered their lives to save their city from the English during the Siege of Calais in 1347, famously depicted in later art and literature as paragons of sacrifice.</note>, who protects his walls, defends his city, his wife, his children, and his life, receives magnificent praise; he is lauded as a noble-minded citizen. One does not notice, however, that the conflict he takes upon himself, and for which he sacrifices himself, concerns him directly; it is his own hearth, his paternal inheritance, and his family that awaken his heroism <term>Heldenmuth: Bravery or a heroic spirit.</term>. 

It is instead in the administration of justice <term>Gerechtigkeit: The fair and equitable application of law.</term>, in the upholding of the laws, in the management of finances, in the application of public funds, the beautification of cities and their security, in useful foundations, and in order and harmony—it is there that I look for the citizen and the patriot.
