# Duties
## of the Knights of the Orient.

---

->I.<-

1. To love the true God, to worship Him, to serve Him, and to be obedient to the sovereign of the land.

2. To abhor slander, lying, and backbiting.

3. To stand by one another, to help everyone in their need, and even to anticipate a brother’s needs before they are asked.

4. To welcome strangers and to practice the virtue of hospitality.

5. To visit the sick, to comfort them, to help them, and to bury the dead.

6. To pray for those who are persecuted and to strive at all times to save and protect innocence.

7. To love all people in general, to flee from the dissolute, and to cleanse oneself of vices.

8. To avoid places where debauchery is practiced and women of ill repute.

9. To be a strict observer of <term>Masonic laws</term><note>original: "maurerischen Geseze." These refer to the specific statutes and traditional regulations that govern the conduct of members within the fraternity.</note>, and to constantly instruct one another with gentleness and correctness.

## Regulations and Jurisdictions.

The <term>jurisdiction</term><note>original: "Gerichtsbarkeit." This refers to the legal authority or power to administer justice and enforce laws within a specific group.</note> of the Masons rests entirely upon mutual consent; coercive power, which is always an emanation of sovereign power, is not in their hands because they have been commissioned neither by the Prince nor by the State. They cannot compel anyone to execute their regulations; as they lack the right to establish <term>positive laws</term><note>original: "positive Gesetze." These are man-made statutes enacted by a government, as opposed to moral or natural laws.</note>, they also do not have the right to impose physical punishments. Their rules and their obligations are merely moral; consequently, the violation of these or the crime itself can only be subject to moral penalties. I would even doubt whether the punishment of dishonor or shame, which they can attach to the good name of the transgressor—be it through striking them from their registers or through expulsion—

# Statutes
## for the Apprentices.

### Article One.

No Brother, of whatever rank or dignity he may be, shall be permitted to propose a <term>profane</term><note>original: "Profanen." In Masonic terminology, a "profane" is a person who has not been initiated into the secrets of the Order; a non-mason.</note> for admission, unless he has first inquired with the utmost care into the character and conduct of the same, for which he will be responsible to the Society upon his honor; and if, contrary to all expectation, a disreputable person should be admitted and thoughtlessly received, then the Brother <term>Godfather</term><note>original: "Pathe." This refers to the sponsor who proposes the candidate and remains responsible for their moral guidance within the lodge.</note> shall likewise be held liable for any offense committed by his <term>proselyte</term><note>original: "Proselyten." A term used here for the new initiate or candidate.</note>; for it is expressly enjoined upon all Brothers to be cautious regarding the profanes they propose.

**Second Article.** As soon as a Brother, who must be at least a <term>Master</term><note>original: "Meister." The third and highest degree in a standard craft Lodge, carrying the authority to propose new members.</note>, has proposed a candidate for admission, the presiding Master shall postpone the deliberation until the next <term>Lodge</term><note>original: "Loge." The formal assembly of Masons.</note> meeting, so that everyone has time to confer and inquire into the conduct of the profane.

**Third Article.** In the following Lodge meeting, the proposing Brother shall request the floor, and ask for the

### Sixth Article.

If the reasons of those who oppose are valid and supported by evidence, the <term>Master</term><note>original: "Meister." The presiding officer and highest authority within the local Lodge.</note> shall return to his chair and say to the whole Lodge: "My Brothers, I hope that no one will concern himself with proposing this or that <term>profane</term><note>original: "Profanen." ### Seventh Article.

After the Lodge has reached a decision regarding acceptance or rejection, the <term>proposing Brother</term><note>original: "vorschlagende Bruder." The member who officially introduced the candidate’s name for consideration.</note> shall notify the accepted or rejected profane of the outcome, without ever revealing the names of those who opposed him; this is done under penalty of <term>expulsion</term><note>original: "Ausschliessung." Removal from the order, often considered the most severe punishment for breaking the vow of secrecy.</note>.

### Eighth Article.

The reasons for rejecting a <term>proselyte</term><note>original: "Proselyten." A candidate seeking admission into the order.</note> must be significant, such as the irregularity of his morals, or if someone in his family has been legally punished; for private matters have no relation to the Society.

### Ninth Article.

Every profane who is proposed in the Lodge shall be announced by the <term>Brother Sponsor</term><note>original: "Bruder Pathen." Literally "Brother Godfather," the member responsible for mentoring the candidate through the initiation process.</note> simply by his first and last name, without any title, in this manner: "The profane [Name] [Name] requests to be accepted as a <term>Mason</term><note>original: "Maurer." A member of the fraternity of Freemasons.</note>," etc.

### Tenth Article.

The <term>Sponsor</term><note>original: "Pathe." Literally "godfather," this refers to the established member who proposes a candidate and takes responsibility for their introduction and conduct.</note> shall be mindful to inform his candidate of the admission costs, which for the first initiation shall never be less than five <term>guineas</term><note>A high-value British gold coin. This substantial fee indicates that the Lodge was intended for men of significant financial means.</note>; for since charity and mutual assistance are the goals of the Brothers, a <term>common treasury</term><note>original: "gemeine Casse." A shared fund used for both the Lodge's charitable works and its operational expenses.</note> must be established to contain the necessary means for these ends. Furthermore, the costs for the initiation arrangements, the candles, and the <term>banquet</term><note>original: "Gastmahls." The ritual meal that follows a meeting, which was a central part of Masonic social life.</note> are considerable, not counting the fees for the <term>serving brothers</term><note>original: "dienenden Brüder." These were members, often from a lower social class, who performed manual or administrative tasks for the Lodge in exchange for a fee rather than paying dues themselves.</note>, which consistently amount to three pounds sterling for each <term>degree</term><note>original: "Grad." The progressive levels of advancement within Freemasonry, typically Apprentice, Fellow Craft, and Master Mason.</note>.

### Eleventh Article.

The sponsor shall be held responsible for delivering the fees to the treasury before the admission; he serves as the guarantor and primary payer, as the Lodge has no claim to demand anything from the candidate himself, but only from the sponsor. The sponsor, in turn, shall inform the <term>proselyte</term><note>original: "Proselyten." A candidate for admission.</note> that, in addition to the aforementioned fees, he is also expected to present a pair of <term>gloves</term><note>original: "Handschue." A traditional gift in Masonic ritual; the white gloves symbolize the purity of heart and the "clean hands" required of a Mason.</note> to every Brother.

# Statutes for the Fellows.

### First Article.

In the <term>Lodge</term><note>The basic organizational unit of Freemasonry, referring here to the formal meeting of members.</note> that is held immediately after a <term>profane</term><note>original: "Profanen." A term used by Freemasons to describe anyone who has not yet been initiated into the order.</note> has been <term>balloted</term><note>A method of secret voting where members use white balls for approval and black balls for rejection.</note> upon, the <term>Brother Sponsor</term><note>original: "Bruder Pathe." Literally "Brother Godfather," the member who proposes a new candidate and takes responsibility for their character.</note> shall propose him once again, and then the acceptance or rejection shall be explicitly declared; the same procedure shall be followed for promotion to the second and all subsequent <term>degrees</term><note>original: "Graden." The progressive stages of Masonic knowledge and rank: Apprentice, Fellow Craft, and Master Mason.</note>, because it must constantly remain the right of every voting Brother—provided it is done with propriety and without partiality—to vote for rejection if he finds it useful for the best interests of the Lodge.

### Second Article.

No <term>serving brother</term><note>original: "dienender Bruder." A member, often from a lower social class, who performed manual or administrative tasks for the Lodge (such as preparing the room or serving meals) in exchange for fees or reduced dues.</note> shall be eligible for admission as a <term>Fellow</term><note>original: "Gesellen." The second degree of Freemasonry, known as the Fellow Craft.</note> unless he has been repeatedly declared capable and worthy, and unless necessity requires it for the security of the Lodge—such as to safeguard our secrets from the profane, or to admit the host of a house where the members meet, or the servant of a <term>Presiding Master</term><note>original: "vorsitzenden Meisters." The head of the Lodge, often called the Worshipful Master.</note>; in such cases, the greatest caution must be exercised, and this favor must be presented to them from the most significant perspective, inasmuch as they are being admitted among their benefactors.

<meta>catchword: Third</meta>

**Third Article.** In the case mentioned above, one must change the <term>password</term><note>Masonic passwords were used to identify a member’s rank or "degree." Serving brothers had specific passwords distinct from the regular membership.</note> of the <term>serving brother</term><note>original: "dienender Bruder." A member who performed labor or service for the Lodge. This article ensures that once such a brother is promoted, his former status as a servant is wiped away to maintain Masonic equality.</note>, and give him the Apprentice and Fellow Craft word; he shall no longer be viewed like the other servants, and no one shall ever be permitted to reproach him for having been one, because it would be better not to elevate him at all than to make reproaches to him.

**Fourth Article.** If, however, a serving brother has rendered important services to the <term>Order</term> and the <term>Lodge</term>, he may be promoted to the <term>Mastership</term><note>original: "Meisterschaft." The third degree of Freemasonry, that of a Master Mason.</note> and even to the higher degrees, but only with great difficulty, because one must be economical with such honors and not squander them on persons who might not recognize their full merit.

**Fifth Article.** It is quite expressly forbidden to promote a <term>profane</term><note>original: "Profanen." An uninitiated person.</note> from the <term>Apprentice degree</term> to the <term>Fellow Craft degree</term> on one and the same day, unless it be in an urgent case, such as due to a journey or another circumstance of a similar nature; and in such a case, the presiding <term>Master</term> shall make the initiate realize the full extent of the favor that has been shown to him.

**Sixth Article.** Before an Apprentice is received as a Fellow Craft, one must consider whether one is inclined and believes it possible to promote him soon thereafter to the Mastership; and if he is deemed worthy of it because he is of a certain <term>rank</term><note>original: "Stande." Referring to social status or class, which heavily influenced promotion speed in 18th-century German Lodges.</note>, then

**Seventh Article.** No assembly shall disperse without the presiding Master first having reminded all brothers of the strict obligation to give <term>alms</term><note>Charity is a fundamental pillar of Freemasonry; collections for the poor were a mandatory part of every meeting.</note>; after which the Brother <term>Treasurer</term> together with a <term>Senior Warden</term><note>original: "Oberaufseher." One of the principal officers of the Lodge who assists the Master in governance.</note> shall pass around the bowl, into which everyone shall contribute according to his will and means. The proceeds of the alms shall thereafter be placed in a special fund, which also receives an increase from various <term>fines</term><note>original: "Strafgeldern." # Statutes
## for the Masters.

---

### First Article.

Here, every person resumes his natural standing after he has been tested in the two preceding degrees according to the <term>Masonic Order</term><note>The structured system of degrees and rituals within Freemasonry.</note>; since receiving these, the <term>Sponsor</term><note>original: "Pathen." A member who recommends and mentors a candidate through the initiation process.</note> is no longer permitted to announce his <term>Candidate</term> other than by the title of "Venerable," a title which he earns through his wisdom and discretion, regardless of his social standing; for with us, a virtuous sage is worth more than the dignity of birth, which is a gift of mere chance.

**Second Article.** When the votes for a candidate for the <term>Master's degree</term><note>original: "Meisterschaft." The third and highest degree of Craft Freemasonry.</note> have been collected and his admission unanimously approved, the duties of the Sponsor cease, and it falls to the <term>Terrible Brother</term><note>original: "furchtbarer Bruder." A specific officer in certain Masonic rites (akin to the "Brother Preparer") responsible for the solemn and sometimes intimidating preparation of the candidate before the ritual.</note> to prepare him more emphatically.

**Third Article.** No Master's initiation shall be held without one or more <term>Apprentice</term><note>original: "Lehrlingsaufnahmen." The first degree of Freemasonry.</note> initiations following it, in order to avoid excessive expense.

**Fourth Article.** The Secretary shall record the first and last names of the new Master and the date of admission by day, month, and year in the <term>secret book</term><note>original: "geheime Buch." A private register maintained by the Lodge to track the progression of its members through various degrees.</note>, so that if delegates from the <term>Grand Lodge</term><note>The governing body overseeing several individual Lodges within a specific jurisdiction.</note> should come annually—as was once the custom—they may take a copy of it and deposit it in the Lodge of the <term>Grand Master</term>.

**Fifth Article.** No brother, regardless of his social standing, may attain any office or dignity within the Order unless he is a Master.

**Sixth Article.** Every Master is eligible by ballot to be elevated to all dignities, even to that of a <term>Presiding Master</term><note>original: "vorsitzenden Meister." The head of the individual Lodge, often referred to today as the "Worshipful Master."</note>, provided there are no brothers of <term>higher degrees</term><note>original: "höhern Graden." Degrees beyond the first three (Apprentice, Fellowcraft, and Master), often referred to as "Appendant" or "High" degrees.</note> who are members of the Lodge; in such a case, the latter must have preference due to their experience, and they cannot be bypassed in the election unless notification is sent to the Grand Lodge (if one exists and the case is significant enough to merit an exclusion).

...ten, of the <term>Scots</term><note>original: "Schotten." In this context, the author refers to "Scottish Masonry" (Ecossais degrees), which were higher degrees in Freemasonry that claimed a legendary—and often mythical—origin in Scotland, though many were developed in France and Germany.</note>, all of which, except for certain variations, contain basically the same <term>principle</term><note>original: "Principium." Taken all together, these regulations of theirs contain nothing of value except for a detailed list of the fraudulent privileges they arrogate to themselves, while pretending to be that which they are not. There is nothing wrong with boasting of one's own country, but it is a very bad thing to claim a lineage from a country to which one does not belong. The Scots of Scotland alone have claims to <term>legal rights</term><note>original: "Gerechtsame." This refers to legitimate historical prerogatives or jurisdiction.</note>; their regulations are wise, but I do not translate them here, because they are foreign to Masonry from the perspective that it presents to us; but I will set down some of those general regulations.

<page-num>200</page-num>

# General
### and
# Old Statutes.

---

### First Article.

Since no one can legally commit themselves to matters with which they are unacquainted, no <term>profane person</term><note>original: "Profan." In Masonic terminology, this refers to a non-member or an uninitiated person; someone "outside the temple."</note> shall be admitted into the Order who has not first been instructed that nothing occurs within it that is contrary to God, religion, the sovereign, the state, or good morals. The word of honor of his <term>godfather</term><note>original: "Pathen." A sponsor or mentor within the Lodge who vouches for the candidate’s character and guides them through the initiation process.</note> shall be given to him as a guarantee. This sponsor shall secure the candidate’s trust with the assurance that he will be absolved of all obligations should he find himself deceived in any of those aforementioned points. By this arrangement, he will have no cause to complain of having been led like a blind man without knowing what was happening.

**Second Article.** If anyone, after his admission, is convicted of a crime regarding any of the points mentioned above—as these are all matters and cases of such gravity that no concealment can be permitted—he shall be publicly dismissed in the <term>Lodge</term><note>The local organizational unit and meeting place of Freemasons.</note> and stripped of his <term>Masonic clothing</term><note>original: "maurerischen Kleidung." This refers to the ritual regalia, specifically the apron and any jewels of office, which symbolize a Mason's standing and membership.</note>

<summary>This page outlines the first two "General and Old Statutes" of the Masonic Order, emphasizing informed consent for new initiates and the strict consequences for moral or legal failings. It establishes the role of the 'godfather' or sponsor in ensuring the initiate that the Order's secrets do not conflict with their civic or religious duties.</summary>

and <term>jewels</term><note>original: "Kleinodien." These are the symbolic medals or insignia worn by members to indicate their rank or office within the Lodge.</note>, if he has any, and shall be forever cast out in shame.

### Third Article.

Since the spirit of peace, concord, and good understanding shall constantly reign among us, one cannot emphasize strongly enough to the <term>candidate</term><note>original: "Candidaten." A person seeking admission into the Order.</note> how strictly it is forbidden to bring any matter into the <term>Lodge</term> that could give cause for strife and discord, such as political opinions, gossip, ambiguous jokes, and so forth. If anyone should act contrary to the present articles, they shall be subject to the penalties established in the regulations under the heading of <term>fines</term><note>original: "Geldbußen."</note>.

### Fourth Article.

Since nothing is more in accordance with nature than to restore people to that <term>equality</term><note>original: "Gleichheit." A core Enlightenment and Masonic value, suggesting that within the Lodge, social hierarchies of the outside world are suspended.</note> to which they were born, no precedence, no distinction, no regard for rank or birth—which are hateful pretensions—shall be tolerated in the Lodge. Thus, if someone is seen to insist upon such things, the <term>presiding master</term><note>original: "vorsizende Meister." The officer who leads the Lodge meetings, often referred to as the "Worshipful Master" in modern English Masonry.</note> must seek to humble him by assigning him the last seat and charging him with the lowest duties for the service of the brothers.

### Fifth Article.

The name <term>Brother</term><note>original: "Bruder."</note> is the only one used within the Lodge; the title "Sir" <note>original: "Herr." This refers to the formal, class-conscious address common in 18th-century society, here replaced by the language of fraternity.</note> is entirely banished from it, as is the use of any language that is foreign and different from that which

**Sixth Article.** The duty of <term>secrecy</term><note>original: "Verschwiegenheit." If it is a brother belonging to the Lodge, one should not wait until he asks for help; one should anticipate his needs;

**Eighth Article.** It would be unjust to humiliate a brother and force him to specify his need and his often unforeseen misfortune—such as a <term>bankruptcy</term><note>original: "Banquerot."</note>, <term>bills of exchange</term><note>original: "Wechsel." It would be possible, but at the same time in my opinion very tedious, to cite a hundred other articles of regulations, statutes, special constitutions of the <term>Lodges</term><note>original: "Logen." The local organized chapters of the fraternity.</note>, and rules of the degrees, which repeat themselves, amount to nearly the same thing, and from which one would fundamentally see no more clearly than from those just cited, how pure the <term>Masonic morality</term><note>original: "Moral der Maurer." An applicant for membership in a Masonic lodge.</note> immediately upon his initiation, and how excellently it is carried out in the instructions and duties imposed upon them. There are few societies whose maxims seem more precisely suited to those essential virtues that adorn humanity and can create its happiness. This public disclosure, which I believed I could permit myself for the benefit of my brothers—in order to rescue them from their bad reputation and to win them supporters and <term>advocates</term><note>original: "Schutzredner." Freemasonry.</note>: why not also to all Masons? <meta>This page continues the author’s exploration of the moral and legal foundations of the Masonic oath. If it is granted that the candidate’s <term>obligation</term><note>original: "Verpflichtung." If a person who has pledged his word based on the assurance of a third party is bound in a valid way to fulfill his promises, then he is no less obligated to carry out the regulations that naturally follow. These are rules which the lodge would certainly not have failed to let him read on the day of his <term>initiation</term><note>original: "Aufnahme." The formal ceremony of being received into the fraternity.</note>.

One can consider Masonic laws from two perspectives: either in view of their general utility, or in view of their private advantage. In the first case, one is quickly finished with the investigation: to preach honor, religion, integrity, compassion, modesty, patriotism, and loyalty means adding nothing to those fundamental concepts <term>primary concepts: the author refers to the basic moral education given to children by their parents or schools</term> which the hand of education writes into the heart of every human being. A logical fallacy where the speaker assumes the truth of the very thing they are trying to prove.</note> that assumes either ignorance and malice in those being exhorted, or an inadequacy in the instruction of those general maxims that are, as it were, the soul of society and the foundation of civil order; it is an unnecessary effort that teaches nothing new and merely imposes long-known duties. In the second case, these same laws provide no greater benefit because they are not consistently uniform and fixed; because, to achieve the good they indicate, the legislators would need to hold a more unquestionable right and power of enforcement in their hands; and because, moreover, they mostly concern matters that are only possible if the Order of Masons were declared by the State to be a privileged body <note>original: "privilegirtes Corps." The sly **Master** delays the assembly with festive songs or with the delicacies of a meal that ends up costing everyone very dearly. There is no time to speak of business; matters are postponed, or if the accounts have already been finalized and prepared by the treasury officials beforehand, the year’s expenses will exceed the income. In a remote border province of a great kingdom, a fanatic citizen, deeply imbued with **Masonic** orthodoxy—one who would sooner resign the civil office by which he lives than lay down the **Gavel** <term>Gavel: Referred to in the German text as a "hammer," this is the symbol of authority used by the Master of a Lodge to govern the assembly.</term> which makes him look ridiculous and which he wields in a most clumsy manner—has committed a hundred such tricks in his life; he has made himself so fluent in mixing "yes" and "no" together that I have seen twenty-five of his own handwritten documents which all contradict one another, and which leave no other means to save his honesty than to deny him any power of judgment; and in doing so, one would actually be doing him a favor. 

It is true that these gushes of sentiment and nonsense are usually the work of the persuasion and the evil hearts of those who surround him: a dissolute nephew without principles, a bad **Mason**, a petty spirit, insidious and insolent, who, along with a few brothers of his ilk, twist the good man's head. In the hands of these mercenary hirelings, the **Royal Art** <term>Royal Art: A traditional term for Freemasonry, emphasizing its status as a noble pursuit of self-improvement and moral architecture.</term> becomes for them only a source of intrigues, of shameful profit, and of bold presumptions; the old uncle, who only yesterday accepted counsel, now leaps from one extreme to the other, and publicly gives the lie to himself and the laws of the **Order**. **Masonic** regulations, what use are you then? One must stand by his brothers—that is the great principle,

...regulations, what use are you then? ## Possible Reform. Conclusion.

I could let it rest there, transcribing word for word the mature considerations that a zealous and insightful brother made regarding this matter three years ago, and which I possess in a very detailed copy of a circular letter which he intended for and sent to an old **Provincial Master** <term>Provinzialmeister</term> <note>The presiding officer of a Provincial Grand Lodge, responsible for a specific geographic region.</note> on September 27, 1764. I will make use of some of his ideas, but without leaning more toward the south than toward the north; my lodge is simply the **Lodge of St. John** <note>original: "Loge des heiligen Johannes" — a term for the basic "Blue Lodge" degrees common to all of Masonry, named for its patron saints.</note>; it is an oblong square having the four quarters of the world at its sides, and the azure vault of heaven as its dome. As a Mason, I reach all these points of the **universe** <note>original: "Weltall"</note>; the zeal and the thought of a **Cosmopolitan** <term>Cosmopoliten</term> <note>A "citizen of the world"—a key Enlightenment ideal emphasizing universal brotherhood over national borders.</note> must fly swiftly around all these boundaries and paint a picture,

...would speak of the **Scottish Degree** <term>Schottengrad</term> <note>The "Scottish degrees" in Freemasonry emerged in the mid-18th century, claiming to offer "higher" knowledge beyond the basic three degrees of Apprentice, Fellowcraft, and Master Mason.</note> of St. Andrew of Scotland. This is the only degree founded upon historical truths and a credible tradition, which captures **Masonry** <term>Maurerey</term> at its origin and leads it toward its moral or physical goal. 

Since a **Master of the Lodge** <term>Logenmeister</term> must necessarily know the science from its very foundation—as he is the one who instructs others—no Mason would reach this post unless he had achieved what is called the "perfection of knowledge." He would, therefore, be drawn exclusively from the **Scottish Guild** <term>Zunft der Schotten</term> <note>The "Scottish Guild" refers here to the collective body of Masons who have attained these higher degrees.</note>, from which the members of the **Grand Lodge** <term>große Loge</term> would also be drawn.

This clause would take the **gavel** <term>Hammer</term> <note>The gavel, or hammer, is the primary symbol of the Master's authority to govern a lodge meeting.</note> and the more important duties away from all those Masons who were not deemed worthy to be admitted into the higher class of the **Order** <term>Orden</term>. 

One would not be eligible for this higher class until one had worked for nine years in the lower classes. The intervals between these classes would also need to be firmly established, after reducing them all to three **degrees** <term>Grade</term>, namely:

## On the perfection of the <term>triple number</term><note>The German *gedritt* refers to the threefold or ternary nature of the number, specifically that nine is the square of three (3x3)</note>, calculated according to the arithmetic properties of the number 9, which are common to no other simple numbers.

One may multiply the number 9 by whatever one
wishes, and the product will always consist of digits
whose units, when added together, give exactly the
sum of 9; for example, 1 and 8 are 9,
2 and 7 are 9 again, and so on up to the <term>cubic number</term><note>The cube of nine is 729; adding its digits (7+2+9) equals 18, and 1+8 equals 9</note>.

Apart from the general joy, in which I take the most lively part, the solemnity that gathers us today gives me the further particular satisfaction of being able to relinquish a position to true merit once more—a position which until now has been held only by zeal, and which I have for a long time, My Brothers, owed solely to your indulgence. If the characteristic qualities of a good Mason are essentially the same as those which distinguish every upright man, free from delusion and prejudice, then I may at least flatter myself [to possess] that degree of integrity <note>original: "Rechtschaffenheit"; a term implying a blend of honesty, moral uprightness, and civic virtue.</note> which is so necessary for good moral order, and without

Dismiss, I pray you, <term>My Brothers</term> <note>original: "M. Br." There you would see that the initiations into the sacred mysteries of the **Good Goddess** <note>Likely a reference to Isis, whose mysteries were a central theme in the "Egyptian" origins of Freemasonry popular at the time.</note> always occurred stepwise and by degrees; that the first trials—despite the fearsome preparations accompanying them—had something childish about them; that the **novitiate** <note>A period of probation or apprenticeship before a member is fully admitted into a religious or fraternal order.</note> lasted a long time; and that each of their ceremonies contained a more important symbol, whose secret meaning was only unlocked after much time as a reward for caution and steadfastness. And if I wished to make a comparison between those rites and what occurs in our own day, it would be easy for me to show you that our first degrees are, so to speak, only a **ladder** <note>original: "Stufenleiter"; literally a "stepladder" or "scale," symbolizing the progressive nature of the Masonic degrees.</note> that one climbs up—

<summary>The Lodge Master justifies the slow, graduated process of Masonic initiation by comparing it to the ancient Egyptian mysteries described in the popular 18th-century literature of the time. I shall certainly take care not to turn to my own advantage the overly general title of the permanent *Master*, which is granted without distinction in the *Letters of Constitution*: my honesty owes you an explanation regarding this, and here it is.

Since the appointed <term>Lodge Masters</term> <note>German: *Logenmeister* — the presiding officer of a Masonic lodge.</note>, by virtue of their office, are members of the <term>Grand Lodge</term> <note>German: *große Loge* — the governing body of Freemasonry in a specific jurisdiction.</note>, and as such must attend the meetings of the said Grand Lodge at least once a year, either in person or through *Deputies*; the Grand Lodge, in order to avoid the inconvenience of a vast multitude of *Masters* gathering annually or at least every three years for the <term>St. John’s Elections</term> <note>German: *Johanniswahlen* — the traditional time for Masonic lodges to elect officers, falling around the Feast of St. John the Baptist on June 24th.</note>, took the measure of appointing permanent *Masters* to reduce the number [of attendees] in such cases. However, while seeking to remedy one inconvenience, it was certainly not their intention to introduce an abuse that would bind the <term>Masons</term> <note>German: *Maurer*.</note> in their <term>freedom</term> <note>German: *Freyheit*.</note> of elections, of changes, and of votes. Rules always precede exceptions; an ancient <term>fundamental rule</term> <note>German: *Grundregel*.</note> has designated <term>St. John’s Day</term> <note>German: *Johannistag*.</note> for the <term>election of the Master</term> <note>German: *Meisterwahl*.</note>; to depart from this <term>law</term> <note>German: *Gesetz*.</note> means to commit an error; to seek to evade it means to abuse one's <term>office</term> <note>German: *Amts*.</note> and to show oneself unworthy of it:

<summary>The speaker argues that the title of "permanent Master" was created by the Grand Lodge for administrative convenience and should not be used to deprive lodge members of their traditional right to elect their leaders annually on St. John's Day.</summary>

You have mistaken courage for ability, unselective phrasing for eloquence, shyness for wisdom, and perhaps occasionally precision for severity: upon a sharper assessment, you would have found me quite devoid of talents, but full of a love to please you, which I shall always retain.

I must not conceal from you that in England, the center of <term>Masonry</term> <note>German: *Maurerey*.</note>, and in those Lodges which follow the arrangement and customs of the <term>London system</term> <note>Referring to the Premier Grand Lodge of England (founded 1717), whose constitutions and rituals became the standard for many European lodges in the 18th century.</note>—such as in Holland, Russia, Prussia, Sweden, Denmark, and almost all of Germany—the custom is for the Master to appoint his first and second Wardens himself. 

Since these two officers are the primary <term>driving forces</term> <note>German: *Triebfedern*—literally "mainsprings," a metaphor comparing the Lodge's operation to the internal mechanics of a clock.</note> that set the work of the Lodge in motion, it seems very natural that he who presides, and who must know each member's merit more precisely, should have the choice of those individuals most capable of assisting him. However, I urge you toward innovation in this matter as little as in any other. The internal organization of every Lodge often depends upon its location; in France, custom has been made into law, and I know that in our Lodge in particular, for more than forty years, the appointment of Wardens has been conducted by ballot. One single rule, which I ask you to establish as unchangeable from this day forward, is that the second Warden

Philanthropy <term>Menschenliebe</term> <note>Literally "love of humanity"; a central tenet of Enlightenment-era Freemasonry.</note> in general, the desire for mutual aid, the harmony of temperaments, the uniformity of opinions, and the relation of needs and means to one another have been the primary bond of humanity since time immemorial; and I can assure you, my brothers, that no one has ever known these better than the **Freemasons**. <meta>This page continues the orator's speech to a new initiate. Having discussed equality on the previous page, he now defines the character of an "upright brother" and argues that the Lodge is the only space where virtues are truly practiced rather than merely discussed.</meta>

An **upright brother** <term>rechtschaffener Bruder</term> — reflect upon the full scope of this word; it portrays at once the **patriot**, the **faithful subject** <note>In the 18th-century context, being a "faithful subject" meant loyalty to one's sovereign or monarch.</note>, the **honest citizen**, the **zealous friend**, and the **man of religion**. Such glorious virtues, with which you also must be familiar, necessarily brought you, my brother, close to the only society wherein one knows how to put into practice principles that, for the rest of the world, are merely chilly chatter and dry dogmas.

This, my brother, is the essence of our **morality**; everything is helpful for maintaining it, and everything about you is suited to it; you will easily be able to judge this for yourself once I explain the **symbols** <term>Sinnbilder</term> <note>These "symbols" or "emblems" likely refer to the physical tools of architecture—such as the square, level, and plumb—displayed in the lodge to represent moral truths.</note> that you have before your eyes.

— — — — — Have I kept my word to you, my brother? And do you not find, after this slight analysis, that the century of **Masonry** <term>Maurerey</term> would indeed be the **Golden Age** <note>A reference to the mythical era of primordial peace and harmony; Masons often believed their rituals preserved the wisdom of this lost age.</note> if all mankind could take part in it? But such is fate, that the use of the best things, when made too common and spread too widely, degrades their essence and reduces their value. <note>The speaker explains why Masonry must remain a secret, exclusive society: he believes that universalizing it would dilute its moral impact.</note>

<summary>The speaker defines the "upright brother" as a paragon of civic and religious virtue and argues that Masonry is the only place where such virtues are truly practiced. He concludes by explaining that the order's exclusivity is necessary to preserve the value and purity of its teachings.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the orator’s address to the new initiate. Enclosed within the narrow circle of a chosen few, the **legacy of the Masons** <term>Beylage der Maurer</term> <note>While "Beylage" often means a supplement or enclosure, here it refers to the specialized tradition or "deposit" of knowledge held by the craft.</note> has been preserved for us in the shadow of discretion and secrecy. Even in happier times, our brothers recognized the necessity of keeping secrets; for, as I have told you, they already formed a kind of separate **guild** <term>Zunft</term> during the **Crusades**, and they carefully hid their organization beneath symbolic veils.

I sincerely regret that I am compelled to tailor the explanation of our customs to the measure of knowledge that the Order permits me to share with you this day. Otherwise, my brother, I would lay before you the entire history of the **Holy War**, and in it, I would soon let you behold the entire progress of this Order. I would move from one Crusade to the next, and linger with pleasure upon that one in which France's holiest hero—one of the most worthy monarchs—proved himself to be our most zealous patron.

From the details that have escaped me, my brothers, you have no doubt already guessed the name of **Saint Louis** <note>Louis IX of France (1214–1270). **Note.** This speech would have sounded ill in the mouth of a man without birth <note>i.e., a commoner or someone not of noble lineage.</note> addressed to a man of rank; it is to be presumed that the **Master** <term>Meister</term> and the **Initiate** <term>Neuaufzunehmende</term> did not yield much to each other in this regard <note>The commentator suggests both men were likely of similar high social standing, making the Master's lecture on equality more acceptable.</note>. Moreover, there reigns in this speech a tone of sincerity that does honor to the Master; he is an honest man who does not deceive the novice with fables; he states the probable origin all at once and, to allow the presumed ultimate purpose to be seen, very skillfully lifts a corner of the curtain.

It is very flattering for me to be able to give you this title, and it will be a delightful pleasure for me to unfold to you in time all the magnificent privileges that are associated with it. -ous than in any other; whereas, on the contrary, our Order breathes nothing but virtue, knows only its principles, prescribes only its teachings to us, and seeks only to plant them in our hearts. You will easily convince yourselves of this truth, my dear brothers, if you follow Masonry <note>original: "Maurerey"</note> in all its points; you will see how precisely it is directed toward this purpose.

The first of our duties is adoration, reverence, and the just tribute of gratitude which man cannot deny the Supreme Being. <note>The "Supreme Being" is a standard Masonic term (often called the Great Architect of the Universe) used to describe God in a way that remains inclusive of different denominational backgrounds.</note>

The second of our obligations is the inviolable loyalty to the sovereign of the land, and the zeal and love which every subject owes to his lawful authority. <note>Masonic constitutions historically emphasize being "peaceable subjects to the Civil Powers" and never being concerned in plots or conspiracies against the state.</note>

Our third rule, finally, binds us to the mutual duties of society: these are, if I may use the expression, the three foundational pillars of our union. ...to raise a question of religion, never to strike up a debate over political matters, or to amuse the assembly at the expense of one’s fellow man: these types of conversation commonly give rise to bitterness, and end by leaving a resentment in the soul of the one who loses the dispute against his victor. <note>The ban on religious and political discussion is one of the oldest "Landmarks" or fundamental rules of Freemasonry, designed to ensure that the Lodge remains a neutral ground for men of all faiths and political persuasions.</note>

Do not imagine, my brothers, that our conversations are therefore drier or less delightful: the freedom that inspires us—freedom, the true property of a Mason <note>original: "Maurer"</note>, the watchword, the soul, the purpose of our society—takes the lead in our meetings; it seasons our joy, it beautifies our secrets, it is the source of our pleasure. But always honorable, always decent, always moderate, it allows us only that wise delight which knows how to enjoy the goods of this life without excess, and without regret—that fury of the reveler—ever poisoning our joys.

Would you believe, my brothers, that a morality <note>original: "Moral"</note> so harsh in appearance, but so sweet in truth, [could face] the censure, the slander, I might almost say the contempt of a crowd of...

Why can we not reveal our teachings and our lovely practices to all mortals for the welfare of humanity! But by a strange fate of the human spirit, good things almost always degenerate by being made common <note>The author expresses a classic Enlightenment-era anxiety: that "making common" (Gemeinmachung) or publicizing sacred truths leads to their vulgarization and loss of value.</note>. Few hearts are good enough, few heads capable enough, few souls pure enough to perceive the value of our secrets and to accustom themselves to our labors: compelled to constantly shroud them with an impenetrable veil for fear of desecration, we expose ourselves to suspicions that dishonor us. Yet in the end, what does the opinion of those who are strangers to us matter? Let us only be jealous of the esteem of our brothers, and then be content to compel the public's respect through outward virtuous deeds, making them blush with shame by a conduct that is inimitable to them.

Masonry <note>original: "Maurerey"</note> itself points us to the means for this, by making the constant exercise of a tender and magnanimous charity <term>Mildthätigkeit: This term implies more than just giving money; it suggests a spirit of active benevolence and "kindliness" toward others.</term> its primary business. <meta>This page continues the speaker's discourse on the ethical duties of the Order, transitioning from the concept of equality to the symbolic construction of the spiritual temple within the individual.</meta>

...spiritual temples, which we strive to erect in our hearts to virtue! The masterpiece of art which the wisest king <note>Referring to King Solomon and the building of the First Temple in Jerusalem, a central allegory in Masonic tradition.</note> built for the Eternal, let it be our model; following his example, let us begin with courage, proceed with wisdom, beautify our works, and above all, tolerate nothing mediocre; a Mason must always strive for perfection.

Let us withdraw our means, our methods, and our laws from the eyes of the uninitiated <term>Ungeweihten: Literally the "unconsecrated"; those outside the Masonic order, often referred to as the "profane."</term>; let a deep mystery hide them from every wicked heart. I cannot impress this upon you earnestly enough, my newly initiated brothers: you must be extremely cautious in this regard. You must not even say where the Lodge was held, nor name those who attended it. Remember your obligations; be zealous, obedient, cautious, and faithful. Thus shall you be true Masons, and we shall congratulate ourselves on having accepted you into our Order, if your future conduct proves you as worthy of it as your countenance <term>Physiognomie: Referring to physiognomy, the 18th-century "science" of judging a person's character or moral standing by their facial features.</term> promises.

## on
## the Admission of a Man of Rank,
## delivered on September 16th in an Apprentice Lodge <term>Apprentice Lodge: A meeting of the First Degree of Freemasonry, where a new member (an Entered Apprentice) is initiated into the Order.</term>.

->My dear Brother!<-

If only you could feel, as vividly as I do, the full value of this flattering title which the Order bestows upon you today as the first reward for the zeal and determination with which you have sought to obtain it: I hope, when I show you the privileges of this title, to convince you that it is no empty word among us; to win you over to our maxims <term>maxims: Fundamental principles or rules of conduct.</term> and precepts, and to prove to you in an indubitable way that Masonry—which has the purpose of making men virtuous—has known how to ingeniously adapt everything to this noble intent, not even excepting the ceremony of your admission, the meaning of which will be explained to you.

...on the other hand, remaining true to the character you possess, which consists above all in honesty and good morals, never departing from our instructions.

A vain meddling has often brought many candidates to us; the hope of encountering supernatural and miraculous things is the sting of a perverse and foolish curiosity. But the enjoyment of the precious advantages associated with the practice of virtue, the constant observation of the duties of humanity, and the assertion of its rights <note>original: "Gerechtsamen"; an archaic term referring to legal rights, privileges, or just claims.</note>—this is the treasure of the wise, the jewel of an honest soul; this is what binds us together, and what we promise you.

Charity <note>original: "Mildthätigkeit"; a central Masonic virtue involving benevolence and material support for the poor.</note> is our particular hallmark, but we know how to temper its zeal and to distinguish between that destitution for which fortune <note>original: "Glück"; used here in the sense of 'fate' or 'happenstance.' The text distinguishes between those who are poor through bad luck versus those poor through their own choices.</note> alone is to blame, and that into which laziness, sloth, and ill conduct have cast a person. To the former, our treasury is open, but our heart is closed against the unseemly outcry of the latter, because a virtuous act must never condone or reward a vice. Our labors, to which you are now <note>original: "ißt"; an archaic spelling of "jetzt" (now).</note> admitted, my dear brother, will [lead] you

...allow you to feel the noble seriousness of the morality that occupies us; the explanation of our signs, our words, our symbols <note>original: "Figuren"; refers to the allegorical diagrams and geometric shapes used in Masonic instruction.</note>, and the ceremonies of your initiation <note>original: "Aufnahme"; literally "reception" or "acceptance" into the order.</note> itself—everything accords with this; everywhere you will see virtue, her temple, and the veneration that we offer her. In our pleasures, our games, and our banquets, even there you will see her presiding, and you will perceive that moderation, sobriety, and respectability which—without denying the needs of nature or detracting from the delights of taste—knows how to prevent that excess which degrades man, debases reason, and makes propriety <note>original: "Wohlstand"; in this 18th-century context, it refers to social decency and "good standing" rather than just financial wealth.</note> blush with shame. 

The profane <term>the profane: derived from the Latin "pro fano" (in front of/outside the temple). In Masonry, this refers to anyone not initiated into the secrets of the order.</term>—as we call everyone who is not a Mason—can more easily excuse himself if he follows the impetuous current of the passions that sweep him away; he does not have the same reins as we do. And it is precisely in this that our society silently gives the world useful lessons, which do not possess the offensiveness of pedantry, and which consist only in the merit of a good example. We are secretive regarding our customs, but the outward conduct of Masons shall constantly bring their principles to light <note>The sentence ends abruptly due to the catchword "legen" (to lay/place), completing the phrase "an den Tag legen" (to bring to light/demonstrate).</note>

manifest <note>continuing from the previous page: "an den Tag legen," meaning to display or bring to light.</note>; this is a permissible self-love <note>original: "Eigenliebe"; in this 18th-century context, it refers to a healthy regard for one's own moral reputation and standing.</note>. Regarding the essential constitution of the Order, you will soon become acquainted with it; it consists primarily in a complete submission <term>submission: "Ergebung" implies a voluntary devotion and yielding to the laws and the leadership of the brotherhood.</term> to the Superiors of the Order and to those whom a higher degree has placed as judges among us. Satisfied with the happiness reserved for us alone, let us wait until someone comes and approaches us to share in it, and above all, let us investigate whether they are worthy of it. We hope, my brother, that we have not been mistaken in you in this regard, and this good opinion will unfailingly be one more motivation for you to continue in your zeal to deserve it. Take your seat. — —

# Fellow Craft Lodge

## Oration

->at<-
->an initiation into this degree,<-
->November 17, 1765.<-

->My dear brothers!<-

Without presuming to boast to you of the special favor and kindness which the Lodge shows you today, in allowing you to pass so quickly to the second class of the builders of the temple <note>original: "Bauleute des Tempels"; a standard Masonic metaphor referencing the builders of King Solomon's Temple as a model for moral self-construction.</note>, I cannot withhold from you that in the earliest times, one had to remain in the apprentice state <note>original: "Lehrlingsstande"; the first stage of Masonic membership.</note> for five years before receiving the Fellow Craft degree: the custom of shortening these intervals arose later, when our labors were restricted to speculations <term>speculations: "Speculationen" refers to Speculative Masonry, where the tools and practices of physical stone-cutting (operative masonry) are used as metaphors for philosophical and moral lessons.</term>; but we do not waive these intervals for everyone without distinction, and those for whom we do waive them, as we have for you, my brothers, must view it as a favor that silently challenges them to make themselves worthy of it.

<vocab>Fellow Craft Lodge, Oration, Initiation, Builders of the Temple, Apprenticeship, Fellow Craft degree, Speculations, Freemasonry</vocab>

...make. Perhaps at first glance you did not notice the differences of this second reception: the same decoration, nothing new in the ceremonial, a small addition to the carpet <note>In 18th-century lodges, the symbols of the degree were often painted on a floorcloth or "carpet" (a precursor to the modern Tracing Board).</note>, one more word—these may seem to announce things that are not very essential to you: meanwhile, this degree, my brothers, offers you objects that invite you to the deepest contemplation, and you will agree with me on this. As you exchange the **rough stone**, the symbol of the **apprentice state**, for the **chiseled cubic stone** <term>cubic stone: also known as the "Perfect Ashlar," it represents the man who has been refined by education and virtue.</term>, the sign of the **Fellow Crafts**, it must immediately be clear to you that this **second degree** already requires more knowledge and more skill for the work than the first; you carried the stones for the building; now you are to hew them: sharpen your tools for this, but remember that this **symbolic language** <note>original: "Bildersprache"</note> concerns only your heart—let that be your first master. Now cast your eyes upon this painting: seven steps, which you climbed in sequence, have led you to the hall; stand firm on the last one, my brothers, to think without ceasing on the things that this symbol contains within itself.

The seven virtues that every true Mason should practice unceasingly. With this explanation, you will no doubt notice that a building whose portal is adorned with such a magnificent inscription is intended to be the sanctuary of wisdom, the temple of happiness, and that you—having offered yourselves as laborers upon it—can reach it in no other way than through the **mystical ladder** <note>The "mystical ladder" likely refers to the theological and cardinal virtues often depicted as rungs on Jacob's Ladder in Masonic iconography.</note> of virtues recommended here; these you must make so much your own, and take so completely into your heart, that they shine forth from every one of your actions.

Not content with merely purifying the soul, the Order also seeks to adorn it with useful knowledge. This knowledge is beneficial in all walks of life and rouses us from that state of slumber in which—due to a failure to exercise the talents received from nature, for which we must account to society—one too often dreams away a lifetime. ## Master Work <term>Master Work: Historically, a "masterpiece" (Meisterarbeit) was the final project a craftsman completed to prove their skill and earn the rank of Master. In Freemasonry, this refers to the ritual and intellectual labor associated with the third degree.</term>.

### Speech
->at<-
->an initiation into this degree,<-
->September 16, 1764.<-

->My dear brothers!<-

The Master’s degree <term>Master’s degree: The third and highest degree of craft Freemasonry, which focuses on themes of mortality, integrity, and the legend of the architect Hiram Abiff.</term>, which the Order grants you today as a privilege, would add little to the initial knowledge of Masonry <term>Masonry: Freemasonry, a fraternal organization that uses the tools and traditions of stonemasonry as allegories for moral and intellectual development.</term> if I were to leave you to your own reflections upon the sight offered by this mournful procession <note>This "mournful procession" (traurige Aufzug) refers to the dramatic ritual of the Master Mason degree, which centers on a symbolic death and funeral ceremony.</note>, and did not help you to unfold its allegory <term>Allegory: A symbolic narrative where characters and events represent deeper moral or spiritual meanings.</term>. You learned at your initiation <term>Initiation: The formal ritual of admission (Einweihung) into the secrets and fellowship of the Masonic order.</term> that our Order

...had as its object, according to its original foundation, the rebuilding of Solomon’s Temple <term>Solomon’s Temple: A central symbol in Freemasonry, representing both the historical structure in Jerusalem and the "spiritual temple" of the human soul and character.</term>; that we, in the continuation of our mystical exercises, still occupy ourselves with this in a moral sense; and you already know the purpose, the plan, the principles, and the study of the Masons <term>Masons: Short for Freemasons, members of a fraternal organization that uses the tools and language of stonemasonry as moral allegories.</term>. The rest, strictly speaking, is only a series of symbols that we require to give grace and variety to the wise morality that is essentially contained in our instruction. Every degree <term>Degree: A stage or level of membership in Freemasonry, each with its own specific rituals and lessons.</term> to which you shall attain will be a higher level of discernment, a greater development of ideas, a new perspective that will show our system in a brighter light.

Today, for wise purposes, the Order occupies your gaze with a mournful procession; everything relates to it: the clothing of the brothers, their conduct, the illumination of the depiction <note>Original: "Gemähldes". This refers to the Masonic "tracing board," a painted cloth or board displaying the symbolic emblems of a specific degree.</note>, the drawings it represents, the ceremony of your reception, the signs I have shown you, the word itself that I have entrusted to you—in short, everything at this moment should remind us of a painful incident which, though not recorded in history, is preserved in the memory of tradition <note>This refers to the Legend of Hiram Abiff, the master architect of Solomon's Temple, whose symbolic death and rebirth form the core ritual of the Master Mason degree.</note>

The one whom the Order regards as its head perished under the repeated blows of the traitors who murdered him; ambition <term>Ambition: In this ritual context, it refers to the "ruffians" who sought the secrets of a Master before they were ready, representing the destructive nature of unearned status.</term> sharpened their dagger, greed plotted their conspiracy, and faithlessness guided their murderous hand. The Father of Masonry <note>A reference to Hiram Abiff, the legendary architect of Solomon's Temple. According to the ritual, he was killed by three workmen for refusing to divulge the Master’s secrets.</note>, whose steadfastness even death could not shake, passes away with his secret as a victim of treachery and his own loyalty. This is the content of the degree that you have just received—a dry, cold, and monotonous content that would give you little satisfaction, my dear brothers, if you did not intend to investigate its secret meaning in every detail.

The loss of the Grand Master <note>Original: "Ordensmeister".</note> undoubtedly deserves our full regret; yet eventually, time erases even the saddest events, and if we did not have a more essential perspective, a solemn narration for the ashes of the Father of Masons would be sufficient. <meta>This page transitions from a general philosophical discussion of human nature and vice into a specific critique of the higher degrees of Freemasonry that proliferated in the 18th century. The catchword "ihrer" at the bottom suggests the next page begins with a phrase like "their source" or "their origin."</meta>

# Notice.

The multitude of degrees that immediately follow the first three <note>The three basic degrees of Craft Masonry: Entered Apprentice, Fellow Craft, and Master Mason.</note> has likewise produced a heap of speeches that are consistent with the fantasies that constitute the essence of these recent inventions; one spares oneself the effort of providing any of that kind, because it would be unbecoming to waste words on subjects whose absurdity and ridiculousness one believes to have already sufficiently demonstrated. Since, moreover, these degrees do not have a fixed form, but change according to the heat of the imagination or the particular advantage of those by whom they are conferred, and are generally of little importance outside of France, the speeches held about them can neither interest nor instruct. <term>Masonry</term> <note>German: *Maurerey*. Referring here to the organizational and ritual structure of Freemasonry.</note> seems to have reached its *farthest limit* <note>original: *nec plus ultra*. A generic term for the various "High Degrees" that claimed a Scottish origin, often emphasizing knighthood or secret knowledge.</note>, where, by a fair estimation, one rejects at least twenty-five <term>chimeras</term> <note>German: *Chimären*. In this context, refers to illusory, unfounded, or nonsensical degrees that exist only in name or imagination.</note> bearing this name, in order to hold fast to the only degree that deserves it and which few people know. Since it is most probable that products are obtained unadulterated when one receives them from

Will the man without knowledge and without gifts be useful for an urgent, tricky, and dangerous business? A business where one must unite experience with the art of guiding minds; where one must make decisions without overstepping the laws; where one must initiate and carry through a negotiation on which the welfare of the state depends; where one must give orders of conduct and shape a people? ...I ask you, when and where is knowledge not necessary? Our Scots <term>Scottish Masons: In the 18th century, "Scottish" degrees in Freemasonry were considered "higher" degrees, claiming to possess ancient, secret knowledge beyond that of standard Masonry.</term> have held every honor and every office with the greatest glory. To be loved by kings, respected by the great, and adored by the people—is this truly the lot of common men? The quiet, domestic virtues draw to us the love of our friends; but great offices, the favor of monarchs, and the happiness of nations demand a man of knowledge—a Scot.

Among the number of sciences that are proper for you, my brothers, I have not explicitly mentioned the hidden study of nature <note>original: "verborgenen Naturkunde." This term refers to "occult" or Hermetic sciences, including alchemy, which were often studied in high-degree Masonic circles to understand the spiritual and physical laws of the universe.</note>—a field that has led so many to ruin for lack of good principles, because they allowed themselves to be guided only by a contemptible self-interest. True sages, on the contrary, have only the happiness of humanity as their focus. This domain has fallen essentially to the Scots; for the sage who despises gold and riches—the value of which exists only in public opinion—shall nevertheless apply himself to imitating nature, to perfecting it, and to discovering the source of its treasures, so that the rest of humanity may benefit from the insight. <note>The speaker is arguing that the Mason does not seek to "make gold" for personal wealth, but to master the secrets of nature to better the human condition.</note> Armed with a patience that nothing can weary, he la- <meta>catchword: beitet (labors/works)</meta>

--- Page 360 ---
<meta>The previous page ended with "All share the same...", leading into the description of shared fates and social bonds. This page continues the Brother Orator’s speech from March 8, 1765, where he uses the historical social structures of Scotland and Ancient Rome as metaphors for the ideal Masonic union.</meta>

...that one finds in Scotland <term>Scotland: While referring to the nation, the speaker is likely alluding to the "Scottish" degrees of Masonry, which claimed an older, more "pure" lineage of loyalty and tradition.</term> the very same connections that were once in use among the ancient Romans. A family from among the common people attaches itself to a lord, adopts his name, binds itself to him through sworn vows, remains forever devoted to him, follows his leadership, undergoes the same misfortunes, and suffers the same changes of fortune.

This connection is more powerful than kinship, more intimate than friendship, and just as inviolable, just as sacred as if it had been ordained by the laws themselves: through it, the weak citizen unites with the nobleman who serves as his support, the farmer with the warrior who defends his harvests, the tradesman with the magistrate who secures his interest and protects his trade: all classes join together for the common good, to make virtues flourish and to honor humanity.

Rome, for a long time the mistress of the world, owed the best part of her glory to those fortunate connections which I cannot sufficiently admire in Scotland. You remember, my Right Worshipful Brothers <note>original: "Hochwürdigen Brüder." A standard honorific for addressing a Masonic lodge or its officers.</note>, the famous and numerous family of the Fabii <note>The Fabii were an ancient Roman patrician clan. They are most famous for the Battle of the Cremera, where the entire family took up arms as a private militia to defend Rome against invaders.</note>, who took it upon themselves to...

<summary>The speaker compares the loyalty found in the Scottish clan system to the patronage systems of Ancient Rome, specifically the Fabii family. He argues that such deep, cross-class social bonds are the foundation of a virtuous society and a model for Masonic brotherhood.</summary>
